## Passage 63: Rudyard Kipling

- **ID:** `raw1_063` | **Seviye:** `A2` | **Konu:** Literature & Biography | **Sayfa:** 76
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_063_rudyard_kipling.mp3` (*US · en-US-AriaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. Rudyard Kipling was born in Bombay, India, in ____. *(İpucu: year)*

2. After returning to India, Kipling worked as a ____. *(İpucu: noun)*

3. In 1907, Kipling won the ____ for literature. *(İpucu: award name)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. Why did Rudyard Kipling go to England when he was 5 years old?

   - A) Because he wanted to become a reporter in London

   - B) Because his father sent him there to go to school

   - C) Because he married an American woman in Devon

   - D) Because he won a prize for his children's stories

2. Which book was about Kipling's old school in Devon?

   - A) The Jungle Book

   - B) Barrack Room Ballads

   - C) Stalky and Co.

   - D) Just So Stories

3. Where did Kipling and his wife live for some time after getting married in 1892?

   - A) Vermont, in the United States

   - B) Bombay, in India

   - C) Devon, in England

   - D) Japan and China


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Kipling visited several Asian countries as well as America during his life.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Kipling did not become famous until he won the Nobel Prize in 1907.


### 2. Reading

The story-writer Rudyard Kipling was the son of an Englishman. Kipling was born on December 30th, 1865 in Bombay, India. When he was 5 years old his father sent him to England. There he went to school at the United Services College in Devon. He wrote about this school later in his book called Stalky and Co. He finished school when he was 17 and returned to India. There, he worked as a reporter and wrote many books. His stories, especially those about soldiers, were very popular and he became very famous at the age of 25. He travelled to India, China, Japan and America and finally went to England. There, he worked on stories of India and wrote a book called Barrack Room Ballads.

In 1892 Kipling married an American - Caroline Balstier. For some time they lived in Vermont in the United States but later returned to Sussex, England. Kipling wrote books for readers of all ages. He also wrote children's stories. Some of them are The Jungle Book (1894), The Second Jungle Book (1895), and Just So Stories (1902). He received the 1907 Nobel Prize for literature.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A. Mark the statements as True (T) or False (F).*

1. 1. Rudyard Kipling lived in India between 1865 and 1870.

   - True

   - False

2. 2. The United Services College is in the U.S.A.

   - True

   - False

3. 3. Kipling worked as a reporter in Sussex, England.

   - True

   - False

4. 4. Barrack Room Ballads is about Kipling's trips to India, China and Japan.

   - True

   - False

5. 5. Kipling was in his twenties when he became famous.

   - True

   - False


*B. Complete the sentences with the information in the text.*

6. 1. Kipling wrote about ____ in his book called Stalky and Co.

7. 2. His stories about ____ made him a famous writer at a young age.

8. 3. Caroline Balstier was Kipling's ____.

9. 4. Kipling wrote The Jungle Book for ____.

10. 5. Kipling received ____ in 1907.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Detail / Chronology]* Which event happened first according to the passage?

   - A) He won the Nobel Prize for literature before publishing The Jungle Book.

   - B) He married Caroline Balstier before he finished school in Devon.

   - C) He returned to India to work as a reporter before marrying Caroline Balstier.

   - D) He wrote Just So Stories before he wrote Barrack Room Ballads.

2. *[Inference]* What can be inferred about Rudyard Kipling from the passage?

   - A) Kipling disliked living in England and never went back there.

   - B) Kipling's experiences in India played an important role in his writing career.

   - C) Kipling only wrote books for young children after 1892.

   - D) Kipling stopped writing after he married Caroline Balstier.

3. *[Reference]* In paragraph 2, what does "them" refer to in "Some of them are The Jungle Book (1894)"?

   - A) readers of all ages

   - B) homes in Sussex and Vermont

   - C) stories about soldiers

   - D) Kipling's children's stories


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Summary: The Life of Rudyard Kipling** (60-80 words): Write a short biography summarizing Rudyard Kipling's life, travels, and major achievements based on the passage.

- **Task 2 — Opinion Paragraph: Why Children's Stories Matter to Everyone** (80-110 words): The passage says that Kipling wrote books for readers of all ages, including children's stories. Do you think adults can enjoy children's stories too? Write a paragraph explaining why or why not.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **reporter** (*muhabir, gazeteci*): He worked as a newspaper reporter when he returned to India.

- **return** (*geri dönmek*): After finishing school in England, Kipling returned to India.

- **famous** (*ünlü, tanınmış*): His stories about soldiers were popular, so he became famous at 25.

- **literature** (*edebiyat*): Kipling received the Nobel Prize for literature in 1907.

- **receive** (*almak, elde etmek*): The writer received an important international award in 1907.

- **of all ages** (*her yaştan*): Kipling wrote stories for readers of all ages.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Kipling'in hayatındaki önemli olayları doğru kronolojik sırayla özetledim mi?

- [ ] Parçadan reporter, famous, literature, receive gibi kelimeleri cümle içinde doğru kullandım mı?

## Passage 64: Video Clips

- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_064_video_clips.mp3` (*UK · en-GB-RyanNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. The Birmingham group Duran Duran only became famous after their ____. *(İpucu: noun phrase)*

2. Besides getting on TV, pop videos can also be bought by people in ____. *(İpucu: plural noun)*

3. Record companies send Jools Holland ____ of pop videos every year. *(İpucu: plural noun)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. How much did Michael Jackson spend to make 'Thriller'?

   - A) 12 million pounds

   - B) 50,000 pounds

   - C) Over 800,000 dollars

   - D) 500 pounds or less

2. Who spends over 12 million pounds a year on videos of new records?

   - A) American TV channels

   - B) British record companies

   - C) Unknown pop groups

   - D) New film directors

3. What is Jools Holland's opinion about British pop videos?

   - A) British videos are much better than American ones.

   - B) American videos are more expensive than British ones.

   - C) Most pop singers are very talented actors.

   - D) New groups never send him any videos.


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Unknown singers can become wealthy very quickly if their videos are shown on MTV.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — According to Jools Holland, most pop singers are naturally good actors.


### 2. Reading

Did you see Michael Jackson's twenty-minute pop video 'Thriller'? He had to spend over $800,000 to make it, and it became very famous all over the world. Unknown singers and groups can become rich in one night when their pop videos get on the American MTV channel. The Birmingham group 'Duran Duran' became famous only after their first video. That made all the difference.

Videos became the biggest thing in the pop music world in the 1960s. British record companies are now spending more than £12 million a year on videos of new records. With these videos, singers and groups get on TV. People can also buy these videos in shops. These two things make singers and groups famous. They can't become famous if they don't make a good video.

Record companies send Jools Holland, the director of a British TV pop music programme, hundreds of videos every year. "I must say that most pop singers are very bad actors," says Jools, "but they don't look bad when record companies spend thousands of pounds on these videos. We've got some excellent film directors in Britain. Their videos are very good - much better than the American ones." Jools Holland gets all kinds of videos: some cost more than £50,000 for three minutes of film; others, from new groups cost £500 or less.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

   - c) is known all over the world

   - d) didn't get on the MTV

2. 'Duran Duran' ____.

   - a) is still an unknown group

   - b) became well-known with their first video

   - c) is a famous video from Birmingham

   - d) was famous before their first video

3. According to the passage, singers and groups become famous when ____.

   - a) they don't make a good video

   - b) people watch their videos on TV

   - c) they make records

   - d) Both (a) and (b) are correct.

4. What does 'they' refer to in "They can't become famous if they don't make a good video"?

   - a) record companies

   - b) new records

   - c) new videos

   - d) singers and groups

5. Which of the following statements is not true about Jools Holland?

   - a) He gets hundreds of videos every year.

   - b) He works for the British TV.

   - c) He directs pop music programmes for the MTV.

   - d) He thinks that not all the pop singers are good actors.

6. What does 'they' refer to in "\"I must say that most pop singers are very bad actors,\" says Jools, \"but they don't look bad when record companies spend thousands of pounds on these videos\""?

   - a) actors

   - b) music programmes

   - c) film directors

   - d) most pop singers


*B*

7. MTV helps unknown singers and groups to become rich and famous.

   - True

   - False

8. Videos have been the biggest thing in the pop-music world for over twenty years only.

   - True

   - False

9. Record companies in Britain spend a lot of money on videos of new records.

   - True

   - False

10. Jools Holland thinks that the American videos are not as good as the British ones.

   - True

   - False

   - True

   - False

   - C) How to become a British film director

   - D) The history of British television programmes

2. *[Detail]* Why do pop singers not look bad in videos despite being poor actors?

   - A) They take acting lessons before filming.

   - B) They only film videos for three minutes.

   - C) Skilled directors and large budgets hide their poor acting.

   - D) American directors help them act better.

3. *[Inference]* What can be inferred from the last sentence of the passage?

   - A) New groups usually make longer videos than famous singers.

   - B) Every pop video needs at least £50,000 to be successful.

   - C) Cheap videos are never shown on British television.

   - D) The cost of making a pop video varies widely depending on the artist.

- **Task 2 — Opinion Paragraph: Music Videos Today** (80-110 words): Do you think music videos are still important for singers today? Do singers need to spend a lot of money to make a good video? Give your opinion with reasons.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **become famous all over the world** (*tüm dünyada ünlü olmak*): The movie became famous all over the world within a few weeks.

- **record company** (*plak şirketi*): Record companies spend millions on videos every year.

- **film director** (*film yönetmeni*): She works as a film director in London.

- **get on TV** (*televizyona çıkmak*): A good video helps unknown singers get on TV.

## Passage 65: Dreams

- **ID:** `raw1_065` | **Seviye:** `A2` | **Konu:** Sleep & Dreams | **Sayfa:** 79, 80
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_065_dreams.mp3` (*US · en-US-JennyNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. Most people dream ____ to six times during the night. *(İpucu: number)*

2. Babies spend almost ____ of their sleep in the REM stage. *(İpucu: noun / fraction)*

3. Dreams give people time to find ____ to some of their problems. *(İpucu: plural noun)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. How often do sleepers enter the REM stage?

   - A) Every 10 minutes

   - B) Every 60 minutes

   - C) About every 90 minutes

   - D) Once a night

2. What happened to the people who stopped dreaming because of sleeping pills?

   - A) They felt more relaxed and energetic.

   - B) They felt bad, worried a lot, and got angry easily.

   - C) They slept for a much shorter time.

   - D) They forgot all of their daily problems.

3. What happened after the people in the experiment stopped taking the sleeping pills?

   - A) They could never dream again.

   - B) They needed more pills to sleep.

   - C) They dreamed for only ten minutes a night.

   - D) They dreamed all night for a few nights to catch up.


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — The first dream of the night is usually the longest.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — People may have more REM sleep when they expect difficult problems the following day.


### 2. Reading

People dream four to six times a night. They dream while they are in the REM (Rapid Eye Movement) stage of sleep. During REM sleep, you breathe faster, and your heart beats faster than in stages one through four. Sleepers go into the REM stage about every 90 minutes. The first dream of the night may last about ten minutes. Each dream gets a little longer. The last dream of the night may be an hour long.

People need their dreams. Younger children spend more time dreaming. Babies spend almost half of their sleep in the REM stage. One experiment showed that everyone needs to dream. Doctors gave some people sleeping pills. These sleeping pills didn't let them go into REM sleep. After a few nights without dreams, they began to feel bad. They became angry easily, they worried a lot, and they wanted to fight with everyone. Then they stopped taking the sleeping pills. They all began to dream all night for a few nights to catch up.

Why do people dream? Dreams give them time to find the answers to some of their problems. If they think they will have difficult problems the next day, they may spend more time in REM sleep the night before. In their dreams, they may find an answer to their problems.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. What does 'them' refer to in "These sleeping pills didn't let them go into REM sleep"?

   - a) the sleeping pills

   - b) the doctors who gave the sleeping pills

   - c) the dreams

   - d) the people who took the sleeping pills

2. The last dream of the night may last ____.

   - a) 10 minutes

   - b) 30 minutes

   - c) 60 minutes

   - d) 90 minutes

3. How many times does a person usually dream a night?

   - a) Two times.

   - b) Ten times.

   - c) Nine times.

   - d) Six times.

4. Who spends the most time dreaming?

   - a) Men.

   - b) Women.

   - c) Babies.

   - d) Older people.

5. What happened to people after a few nights without dreams?

   - a) They became angry easily.

   - b) They began to feel bad.

   - c) They wanted to fight with everyone.

   - d) All of the above.

6. Why do we dream?

   - a) We dream to find answers to our problems.

   - b) We dream so we won't go into REM sleep.

   - c) We dream so we won't need sleeping pills.

   - d) All of the above.


*B*

7. During REM sleep, your heartbeat and breathing slow down.

   - True

   - False

8. People don't dream when they take sleeping pills.

   - True

   - False


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Author's Purpose]* What is the main purpose of the passage?

   - A) To explain the REM sleep stage and why dreaming is necessary

   - B) To warn readers never to use sleeping pills

   - C) To show why babies sleep longer than adults

   - D) To teach people how to remember their dreams

2. *[Inference]* What did the doctors' experiment demonstrate?

   - A) Sleeping pills always make people dream more.

   - B) Only children need REM sleep to stay healthy.

   - C) Missing dreams negatively affects people's mood and behavior.

   - D) Adults do not enter REM sleep after age thirty.

3. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In paragraph 2, what does the phrase "to catch up" mean?

   - A) to wake up earlier than usual

   - B) to make up for lost dreaming time

   - C) to stop taking medicine forever

   - D) to breathe more slowly during sleep


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Guided Summary: Why We Dream** (60-80 words): Summarize what the passage says about REM sleep, the sleeping pill experiment, and why people dream.

- **Task 2 — Opinion Paragraph: The Value of Good Sleep** (80-110 words): How important is good sleep in your daily life? How do you feel when you do not sleep well? Write a short paragraph explaining your experience.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **stage of sleep** (*uyku evresi / aşaması*): During the deep stage of sleep, the body rests completely.

- **sleeping pill** (*uyku hapı*): Some people take sleeping pills when they cannot fall asleep.

- **catch up** (*arayı kapatmak, yetişmek*): After missing two classes, I studied hard to catch up.

- **do an experiment** (*deney yapmak*): Scientists did an experiment to understand how dreams work.

- **become angry easily** (*kolayca sinirlenmek*): When I am tired, I become angry easily.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] REM uykusu, rüya süreleri ve uyku hapı deneyinin sonucunu eksiksiz özetledim mi?

- [ ] Sebep-sonuç bağlaçlarını (because, so, after) doğru kullandım mı?

- [ ] Parçadaki temel kelimeleri (stage of sleep, experiment, catch up vb.) doğru bağlamda kullandım mı?

- [ ] Cümlelerimin dil bilgisi, yazım ve kelime sayısı hedeflerine uygun olduğunu kontrol ettim mi?

## Passage 66: Oranges

- **ID:** `raw1_066` | **Seviye:** `A2` | **Konu:** Food & Agriculture | **Sayfa:** 81, 82
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_066_oranges.mp3` (*AU · en-AU-NatashaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. There were already orange trees about ____ million years ago. *(İpucu: number)*

2. The orange tree most likely originated in ____. *(İpucu: proper noun)*

3. When the Spanish planted orange trees in the Americas, they took them to ____ first. *(İpucu: proper noun)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. What does an orange tree have?

   - A) Large red flowers with no smell

   - B) Flowers only after the fruit falls

   - C) Small white flowers that smell very sweet

   - D) Dull brown leaves and bitter flowers

2. How did Europeans learn to grow oranges?

   - A) From farmers in Asia and the Middle East

   - B) Directly from the Spanish in Florida

   - C) From paintings in South America

   - D) From wild trees growing in Europe

3. Why is the colour "orange" named after the fruit in English?

   - A) Because the flowers are bright orange

   - B) Because the fruit has a very beautiful colour

   - C) Because Spanish explorers named the colour first

   - D) Because oranges were first grown in England


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Oranges twenty million years ago were much larger than modern oranges.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — People in China started growing orange trees thousands of years ago.


### 2. Reading

Everybody loves oranges. They are sweet and juicy. They are in sections, that is, separate parts, so it is easy to eat them. Some oranges do not have any seeds, i.e., parts which grow into a new plant. Some have a thick skin but others have a thin skin.

The orange tree is beautiful. It has a lot of shiny green leaves. The small white flowers smell very sweet. An orange tree has flowers and fruit at the same time.

There were orange trees twenty million years ago. The oranges were very small, not like the ones today. The orange tree probably came from China. Many different kinds of wild oranges grow there today. In other words, these oranges grow in nature. The Chinese started to raise, or grow, orange trees around 2400 B.C.; Chinese art has lovely old pictures of oranges and orange trees.

Farmers in other parts of Asia, such as India and Pakistan, and the Middle East, learned to raise oranges from the Chinese. Then they taught the Europeans. The Spanish planted orange trees in North and South America, called the New World. They took them to Florida first. Oranges are a very important crop (farm product) in Florida today.

"Orange" is both a fruit and a colour. The colour of oranges is very beautiful. Therefore, in English we use the name of the fruit for the colour.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. Guess the meaning of 'sections' in "They are in sections, that is, separate parts, so it is easy to eat them."\na) Clue:\nb) Sections are ____.

2. Guess the meaning of 'seeds' in "Some oranges do not have any seeds, i.e., parts which grow into a new plant."\na) Clue:\nb) Seeds are ____.

3. Guess the meaning of 'wild' in "Many different kinds of wild oranges grow there today. In other words, these oranges grow in nature."\na) Clue:\nb) Where do wild oranges grow?

4. Guess the meaning of 'raise' in "The Chinese started to raise, or grow, orange trees around 2400 B.C."\na) Clue:\nb) To raise means to ____.

5. Guess the meaning of 'other parts of Asia' in "Farmers in other parts of Asia, such as India and Pakistan, and the Middle East, learned to raise oranges from the Chinese."\na) Clue:\nb) India and Pakistan are ____.

6. Guess the meaning of 'the New World' in "The Spanish planted orange trees in North and South America, called the New World."\na) Clue:\nb) The New World is ____.

7. Guess the meaning of 'crop' in "Oranges are a very important crop (farm product) in Florida today."\na) Clue:\nb) The word 'crop' means ____.


*B*

8. What does 'them' refer to in "They are in sections, that is, separate parts, so it is easy to eat them"?

   - a) skins

   - b) seeds

   - c) oranges

9. What does 'It' refer to in "The orange tree is beautiful. It has a lot of shiny green leaves"?

10. What does 'ones' refer to in "The oranges were very small, not like the ones today"?

11. What does 'there' refer to in "The orange tree probably came from China. Many different kinds of wild oranges grow there today"?


*C*

12. Write down three characteristics of an orange.

13. How did the United States get orange trees?

14. Why do English-speaking people use the name of the fruit 'orange' for the colour?


*D*

15. Some oranges do not have ____.

   - a) sections

   - b) flowers

   - c) seeds

   - d) a skin

16. Which of the following is not true about the orange tree?

   - a) It has flowers and fruit in the same season.

   - b) Its flowers come after its fruit.

   - c) Its flowers are small.

   - d) You can find paintings of orange trees in China.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Main Idea]* What is the passage mainly about?

   - A) How to cook with oranges in Asia

   - B) Why Florida farmers prefer thick-skinned oranges

   - C) The characteristics, history, and name of the orange

   - D) The differences between wild plants and farm crops

2. *[Detail]* Which path shows the historical spread of orange farming described in the passage?

   - A) China -> Florida -> India -> Europe

   - B) China -> Asia & Middle East -> Europe -> Americas

   - C) Middle East -> China -> Europe -> Pakistan

   - D) Europe -> China -> Florida -> South America

3. *[Inference]* What can be inferred from paragraph 3 about oranges in China?

   - A) Oranges were only used as a painting subject, not food.

   - B) Wild oranges disappeared from China after 2400 B.C.

   - C) Oranges were originally called "Florida" in ancient China.

   - D) Oranges have been valued in China for thousands of years.


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Guided Summary: The Story of Oranges** (60-80 words): Write a short summary describing what oranges are like and how orange farming spread around the world.

- **Task 2 — Opinion Paragraph: My Favourite Fruit** (80-110 words): What is your favourite fruit, and why do you like it? How do people usually eat or use this fruit in your country? Write a short paragraph.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **wild** (*yabani, doğada kendiliğinden yetişen*): Wild strawberries grow naturally in the forest.

- **raise** (*yetiştirmek (bitki veya hayvan)*): Farmers raise different vegetables on this land.

- **crop** (*tarım ürünü, mahsul*): Wheat is the most important crop in this region.

- **thick skin** (*kalın kabuk*): This orange has a very thick skin, so it is hard to peel.

- **at the same time** (*aynı anda*): The tree has flowers and fruit at the same time.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Portakalın özelliklerini, kökenini ve dünyaya yayılışını doğru sırayla anlattım mı?

## Passage 67: The Postage Stamp

- **ID:** `raw1_067` | **Seviye:** `A2` | **Konu:** Postal Service History | **Sayfa:** 83, 84
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_067_the_postage_stamp.mp3` (*UK · en-GB-SoniaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. Rowland Hill had the idea of a postage stamp with ____ on the back. *(İpucu: noun)*

2. In 1874, a German organized the ____ to solve international mail problems. *(İpucu: organization name)*

3. Today, the offices of the Universal Postal System are located only in ____. *(İpucu: country)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. Why was it difficult to send an international letter before the postage stamp?

   - A) Post offices refused to deliver any letters

   - B) Both the sender and the receiver had to pay separately

   - C) Stamps were too expensive for most people

   - D) Letters could only travel inside Great Britain

2. What is special about the Penny Black?

   - A) It was the most expensive stamp ever sold

   - B) It was invented in Germany in 1874

   - C) It was one of the first two stamps made in 1840

   - D) It was used only for international letters

3. How did countries solve international mail problems through the UPS?

   - A) They agreed to accept letters with prepaid stamps from other members

   - B) They stopped selling their own postage stamps

   - C) They moved all their post offices to Switzerland

   - D) They asked receivers to pay for international letters again


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Stamp collecting is one of the most popular hobbies globally.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Rowland Hill was over fifty years old when he invented the postage stamp.


### 2. Reading

Before the postage stamp, it was difficult to send a letter to another country. The sender paid for the letter to travel in his or her own country. Then the person in the other country paid for the other part of the trip. If a letter crossed several countries, the problem was bigger.

Rowland Hill, a British teacher, had the idea of a postage stamp with gum on the back. The British Post Office made the first stamps in 1840. They were the Penny Black and the Twopence Blue. A person bought a stamp and put it on a letter. The post office delivered the letter, or took the letter to the person. When the person got the letter, it was prepaid. That is, the sender paid for it earlier.

Postage stamps became popular in Great Britain immediately. Other countries started making their own postage stamps very quickly. However, there were still problems with international mail. Some countries did not want to accept letters with the stamps of other countries. Finally, in 1874 a German organized the Universal Postal System (the UPS). Each country in the UPS agreed to accept letters with prepaid postage from the other members. Today the offices of the UPS are only in Switzerland. Almost every country in the world is a member of this organization. It takes care of any international mail problems.

Today post offices in every country sell beautiful stamps. Collecting stamps is one of the most popular hobbies in the world, and every stamp collector knows about the Penny Black and the Twopence Blue.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. The word volcano comes from the island of Vulcano, located just north of ____. *(İpucu: place name)*

2. A volcano's chimney goes down to a liquid deep in the earth called ____. *(İpucu: noun)*

3. Lava and rock often collect around the vent and form the volcano's ____. *(İpucu: noun)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. Where does the word 'volcano' come from?

   - A) It was named after a famous Sicilian scientist

   - B) It means a mountain filled with gas in Italian

   - C) It comes from the island of Vulcano, once thought to be Vulcan's home

   - D) It describes the shape of a deep underground chimney

2. What comes out of a volcano's vent?

   - A) Hot lava, pieces of rock, and large amounts of gas

   - B) Cold water, magma, and volcanic ash

   - C) Deep earth, chimneys, and farmland soil

   - D) Only solid rock and liquid magma

3. How do scientists group volcanoes?

   - A) By their height and beauty

   - B) By how many cities they have destroyed

   - C) By the age of the island they sit on

   - D) By the type of vent and kind of explosion


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Scientists now know almost everything about how volcanoes work.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Volcanoes have caused damage to farmland and forests in the past.


### 2. Reading

Earthquakes and volcanoes frighten people but they also give them great pleasure. Although volcanoes are mostly beautiful mountains, they have destroyed cities, forests and farmland throughout history. The word volcano comes from the name of the island of Vulcano, just north of Sicily. In classical times, people thought it was the home of Vulcan - the god of destruction. Science has improved greatly. However, we still know very little about volcanoes.

A volcano is a kind of chimney, or vent. This chimney goes down to a liquid deep in the earth, called 'magma'. Three types of material come out of this vent: a hot liquid (lava), pieces of rock and great quantities of gas. The lava and rock often collect around the vent and form the volcano's 'cone'.

Scientists often classify, i.e. group, volcanoes according to the type of vent and kind of explosion.

*A*

3. Write the meaning of 'magma' in "This chimney goes down to a liquid deep in the earth, called 'magma'."

4. Write the meaning of 'lava' in "Three types of material come out of this vent: a hot liquid (lava), pieces of rock and great quantities of gas."

5. Write the meaning of 'classify' in "Scientists often classify, i.e. group, volcanoes according to the type of vent and kind of explosion."


*B*

6. What does 'them' refer to in "Earthquakes and volcanoes frighten people but they also give them great pleasure."?

7. What does 'they' refer to in "Although volcanoes are mostly beautiful mountains, they have destroyed cities, forests and farmland throughout history."?

8. What does 'it' refer to in "The word volcano comes from the name of the island of Vulcano, just north of Sicily. In classical times, people thought it was the home of Vulcan - the god of destruction."?


*C*

9. Although science has developed greatly, we still do not know much about volcanoes.

   - True

   - False

10. 'Magma' comes out of a volcano's 'vent'.

   - True

   - False

11. Pieces of rock and large quantities of gas form the 'cone'.

   - True

   - False

12. The type of volcano depends on the kind of vent and explosion.

   - True

   - False


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

   - B) Volcanoes are both attractive and dangerous natural features

   - D) Volcanoes no longer destroy forests or farmland

   - C) colors

## Passage 69: Containers

- **ID:** `raw1_069` | **Seviye:** `A2` | **Konu:** Everyday Objects & Materials | **Sayfa:** 86, 87, 88
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_069_containers.mp3` (*US · en-US-AriaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. A can is a ____ container that is usually cylindrical in shape. *(İpucu: material)*

2. Unlike a bottle, a jar has a ____ and no neck. *(İpucu: noun phrase)*

3. A bag is a ____ container, meaning it is soft and movable. *(İpucu: adjective)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. Why are cartons not very durable?

   - A) Because they are always made of glass

   - B) Because they have push-button tops

   - C) Because they are usually made of heavy paper

   - D) Because they are used by chemists and biologists

2. According to the passage, what can bottles hold besides beverages?

   - A) Paint and heavy metal tools

   - B) Liquid foods like soup and various chemicals

   - C) Jewelry and eggs

   - D) Wastebaskets and paper labels

3. How do people close bags?

   - A) By folding, tying, or knotting them

   - B) By using screw-on lids or hinges

   - C) By cutting them with a utensil

   - D) By placing caps that snap on


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Jars can be washed and reused.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — The speaker describes containers used for cooking food in detail.


### 2. Reading

We can find containers in our homes, schools, and places of work. For example, food and nonfood products are sold in containers. A favourite container of students and teachers is the wastebasket. Of course, containers are an important part of many professions: painters, doctors, biologists, photographers, chemists, and others use many kinds of specialized containers.

In this short article, it is not possible to discuss all kinds of containers. Therefore, let us look at some of the simple and basic containers. We will name them, identify their shapes and the materials they are made of, and say a few words about lids and tops.

Cans

A can is a metal container. It is usually cylindrical in shape, and may have a paper label on the outside. The name of the product is printed on the label or on the metal itself. Cans open in different ways, depending on the product. We need a can opener to open some cans; this utensil cuts the metal. Paint cans have lids. Beverage cans have a pop top or a ring top. Spray cans have a push-button top. Cans are durable containers. In other words, they are strong and long-lasting.

Boxes and Cartons

Boxes and cartons are similar containers. Cartons are usually made of cardboard (heavy paper) and, as a result, are not very durable. Boxes can be made of cardboard, paper, wood, metal, or plastic. Boxes and cartons have rectangular or square sides. Some of these containers, such as jewelry boxes and egg cartons, have tops that open and close with hinges. Other boxes and cartons have removable tops (i.e., you can take these tops off).

Jars

A jar is a glass or ceramic container. It has a wide mouth (top opening) and no neck. Some jars have tops or covers called screw-on-lids and others have lids that fit inside the mouth. Some jars (e.g., jars you see in supermarkets) are very practical because they hold many different products, and because we can clean them and use them again. Jars are durable but breakable. In other words, they are easy to break by accident.

Bottles

A bottle, like a jar, is a container that is usually made of glass. These days, however, plastic bottles are also very common. Bottles are different from jars in one important way: a bottle has a small mouth and a neck, but a jar has a wide mouth and no neck. Bottles have caps or tops which either screw on or snap on. Bottles hold beverages (juice, soda, etc.) and other liquid food such as soup. They are also used to contain photographic, industrial, and medical chemicals.

Bags

A bag is a flexible container. That is, it is soft and movable. Many bags are made of paper, foil, or thin plastic. Such bags are not durable containers - we usually throw them away. We close bags in several ways, for example, by folding them, by tying them with something, and, in the case of plastic bags, by knotting them. Bags come in many sizes and contain many different products.

The containers mentioned in this article are just a few of many hundreds of different containers. We have not talked about tubes, tubs, bins, baskets, vats, vases, casks, drums, flasks, trays, or tanks. We have not mentioned containers that we use in cooking and serving food. We have not talked about containers by profession: painters use cans for paint; photographers use bottles and trays for chemicals; chemists use beakers and test tubes. What containers do you have around you in your everyday life? What containers do you use in your profession? Can you imagine a world without containers?


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. What does 'them' refer to in "Therefore, let us look at some of the simple and basic containers. We will name them, identify their shapes and the materials they are made of, and say a few words about lids and tops."?

2. What does 'this utensil' refer to in "We need a can opener to open some cans; this utensil cuts the metal."?

3. What does 'others' refer to in "Some jars have tops or covers called screw-on-lids and others have lids that fit inside the mouth."?

4. What does 'They' refer to in "Bottles hold beverages (juice, soda, etc.) and other liquid food such as soup. They are also used to contain photographic, industrial, and medical chemicals."?

5. What does 'Such bags' refer to in "Many bags are made of paper, foil, or thin plastic. Such bags are not durable containers - we usually throw them away."?


*B*

6. Guess the meaning of 'durable' in "Cans are durable containers. In other words, they are strong and long-lasting."

7. Guess the meaning of 'cardboard' in "Cartons are usually made of cardboard (heavy paper) and, as a result, are not very durable."

8. In "Other boxes and cartons have removable tops (i.e., you can take these tops off).", if a top is removable:

   - a) it belongs to an egg carton

   - b) you cannot open or close it

   - c) you can take it off the box

   - d) it is very strong

9. Based on "Some jars have tops or covers called screw-on-lids...", a screw-on-lid is a kind of ____.

10. Based on "Bottles hold beverages (juice, soda, etc.) and other liquid food such as soup.", 'soup' is a type of ____.

11. Guess the meaning of 'flexible' in "A bag is a flexible container. That is, it is soft and movable."


*C*

12. What are the types of cans mentioned in the passage? (Write two of them.)

13. Why are jars easy to break?


*D*

14. This passage is mainly about

   - a) containers in supermarkets

   - b) five basic containers

   - c) containers used by chemists and photographers

   - d) glass containers

15. According to the passage, bottles are usually made of

   - a) paper or plastic

   - b) glass or cardboard

   - c) metal or wood

   - d) glass or plastic

16. Boxes, jars, and bottles open and close with different

   - a) materials

   - b) containers

   - c) tops

   - d) necks

17. One important difference between a jar and a bottle is

   - a) the material it is made of

   - b) the size of the mouth

   - c) the label

   - d) the size of the container


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Detail]* What does the author mean by saying that jars are "durable but breakable"?

   - C) They last a long time if handled carefully, but can shatter easily

   - D) They are made of cardboard and tear quickly

2. *[Inference]* Which of the following containers is usually thrown away after use?

   - A) Can

   - B) Thin plastic bag

   - C) Glass jar

   - D) Metal box

   - A) To criticize factories for making too many plastic bottles

   - B) To teach readers how to recycle old cardboard cartons

   - D) To make readers aware of how important containers are in daily life


### 3. Writing

- **Task 2 — Opinion Paragraph: Reusable vs. Disposable Containers** (80-110 words): Many containers, such as thin plastic bags and cardboard cartons, are thrown away after one use. Do you think people should try to use reusable containers instead? Give examples.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **durable** (*dayanikli, uzun omurlu*): Metal cans are durable enough to protect food for years.

- **breakable** (*kirilabilir*): Glass jars are useful, but they are breakable if you drop them.

- **flexible** (*esnek, bukulgen*): This backpack is made of flexible material so you can fold it easily.

- **practical** (*kullanisli, pratik*): A glass jar is very practical for storing dry rice and pasta.

- **specialized** (*ozel amacli, uzmanlasmis*): Chemists use specialized containers like beakers and test tubes in the laboratory.

## Passage 70: Luminous and Non-Luminous Bodies

- **ID:** `raw1_070` | **Seviye:** `A2` | **Konu:** Light & Energy | **Sayfa:** 89, 90
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_070_luminous_and_non_luminous_bodies.mp3` (*UK · en-GB-RyanNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. We can see the Moon in the sky only because it ____ the light from the Sun. *(İpucu: verb)*

2. Without sunlight, the temperature on the Moon can drop to ____ below zero. *(İpucu: temperature)*

3. Materials that allow light through without showing the objects behind them are called ____. *(İpucu: noun phrase)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. What does the passage say about the Moon?

   - A) It produces both light and heat energy.

   - B) It creates a small amount of light at night.

   - C) It does not produce light and only reflects sunlight.

   - D) It becomes luminous when temperatures drop below zero.

2. Why are windows made of clear glass?

   - A) They block all light and hide everything behind them.

   - B) They let light pass through so we can see clearly through them.

   - C) They allow light through but prevent us from seeing objects behind them.

   - D) They are always made of iron or thick plastic.

3. What is true about opaque substances?

   - A) Neither light nor sight can pass through them.

   - B) Only sunlight can pass through them.

   - C) We can see shadows clearly behind them.

   - D) They reflect heat better than transparent materials.


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — An electric lamp and a fire are both examples of luminous bodies.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — A piece of iron produces a small amount of heat and light on its own.


### 2. Reading

Light, like heat, is a form of energy and there is a close connection between the two. Most bodies (i.e. objects) which emit, or send out, light also emit heat. The Sun, for example, emits both light and heat energy. So does an electric lamp or a fire. We can see these bodies because they give out light. They are called luminous bodies. The Moon, however, is not a luminous body because it does not produce light. We can see the Moon in the sky only because it reflects the light from the Sun. This means the Sun sends out light to the Moon and the Moon sends the Sun's light back to Earth. So, the Moon is a good example of a non-luminous body. Because it is non-luminous, it does not produce heat. When there is no sunlight, it is very cold there. The temperature can be 129°C below zero. A piece of iron or glass is also non-luminous.

What are transparent, translucent and opaque substances?

So, light is an important source of energy. We can see things only because there is light from luminous bodies. But light cannot pass through every substance. For example, we can see through clear glass or water but we cannot see anything behind a wall. Substances such as clear glass, water or some kinds of plastic are called transparent substances. That is, light can pass through and we can see what is on the other side. These transparent substances can be used for different purposes. For example, windows are made of glass because we want to be able to see what is outside. But some substances are not clear; in other words, they are not transparent. We cannot see the objects behind them but we can only see the light. These are called translucent substances. Some types of glass are like this. There are also substances called opaque. Light cannot pass through such substances and we cannot see anything behind them.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. Luminous bodies produce heat.

   - True

   - False

2. An electric lamp is a luminous object.

   - True

   - False

3. The Sun reflects light.

   - True

   - False

4. We can see the Moon because it produces light.

   - True

   - False

5. The Moon is very cold when it does not receive sunlight.

   - True

   - False

6. Opaque substances are not transparent.

   - True

   - False


*B*

7. What does 'emit' mean in "Most bodies (i.e. objects) which emit, or send out, light also emit heat"?


*C*

8. What does 'these bodies' refer to in "We can see these bodies because they give out light"?

   - a) the Sun, an electric lamp, a fire

   - b) the Sun

   - c) an electric lamp, a fire

9. What does 'it' refer to in "The Moon, however, is not a luminous body because it does not produce light"?

   - a) the sky

   - b) the Moon

   - c) the Sun

10. What does 'them' refer to in "But some substances are not clear; in other words, they are not transparent. We cannot see the objects behind them but we can only see the light"?

   - a) substances

   - b) transparent substances

   - c) plastic

11. Which of the following is true about non-luminous bodies?

   - a) They produce heat and light.

   - b) They only produce light.

   - c) They don't produce heat or light.

   - d) They produce light but not heat.

12. Which of the following is true about transparent substances?

   - a) They are clear substances.

   - b) We can't see through them.

   - c) They are translucent.

   - d) Light cannot pass through them.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Main Idea]* What is the main focus of the passage?

   - A) Why the Moon is colder than Earth during winter

   - B) How light is produced and how it interacts with different materials

   - C) How glass windows are manufactured for modern buildings

   - D) Why heat energy is more useful than light energy

2. *[Inference]* If light enters a room through a bathroom window but you cannot see the trees outside, what can be inferred about that glass?

   - A) It is a luminous body.

   - B) It is an opaque substance.

   - C) It is a translucent substance.

   - D) It is a transparent substance.

3. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In the passage, the phrase 'give out' is closest in meaning to:

   - A) absorb

   - B) stop using

   - C) reflect back

   - D) send out


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Guided Summary: Light and Substances** (60-80 words): Write a short summary explaining the difference between luminous and non-luminous bodies, as well as the three types of substances mentioned in the passage.

- **Task 2 — Everyday Uses of Light Materials** (80-110 words): Write a paragraph about how we use transparent, translucent, and opaque materials in a house and why each type is important.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **emit** (*yaymak, salmak*): Stars emit light and heat across space.

- **reflect** (*yansıtmak*): Calm water reflects the mountains like a mirror.

- **transparent** (*saydam, şeffaf*): The bottle is made of transparent plastic so you can see the water inside.

- **translucent** (*yarı saydam*): The bathroom door has translucent glass for privacy.

- **opaque** (*opak, ışık geçirmeyen*): Wood is an opaque material that blocks sunlight.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Işık yayan ve yaymayan cisimler arasındaki farkı net açıkladım mı?

## Passage 71: Coal

- **ID:** `raw1_071` | **Seviye:** `B1` | **Konu:** Coal & Energy | **Sayfa:** 91, 92
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_071_coal.mp3` (*US · en-US-JennyNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. At least 3,000 years ago, people in China used coal for ____. *(İpucu: verb phrase)*

2. ____ has the highest carbon content among the four kinds of coal and gives the most heat. *(İpucu: noun)*

3. Existing underground coal supplies will likely last for about ____. *(İpucu: time period)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. What are the two advantages of coal compared to petroleum and natural gas?

   - A) It produces less smoke than natural gas and petroleum.

   - B) It lies closer to the surface and is more abundant than petroleum and natural gas.

   - C) It can be turned into liquid fuel more easily than petroleum.

   - D) It was discovered in Europe earlier than in Asia.

2. Why is anthracite the most preferable kind of coal for homes?

   - A) It is the cheapest and easiest kind of coal to find.

   - B) It contains the highest amount of moisture.

   - C) It burns slowly and keeps a steady, uniform fire.

   - D) It contains less than 75 percent carbon.

3. How does coal's importance today compare with the late 19th century?

   - A) Coal, petroleum, and natural gas are equally important today.

   - B) Coal is still the single most important fuel in the world.

   - C) Natural gas has completely replaced coal in steam engines.

   - D) Petroleum is used less today than it was in the 12th century.


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — The invention of the steam engine led to a major increase in coal use.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Anthracite is the most abundant of the four kinds of coal.


### 2. Reading

Coal is a hard black substance which we take from below the surface of the earth and burn as fuel. People have known the burning property of coal for centuries. We know, for example, that the Chinese used it for smelting copper (Cu) at least 3000 years ago. In Europe, they started using coal in the 12th century in England. But people used coal the most after the invention of the steam engine, during the 18th and 19th centuries. Towards the end of the 19th century, coal was the leading fuel of the world. Today, coal, petroleum and natural gas are fuels of equal importance. One advantage of coal is that we can find it closer to the surface than petroleum and natural gas. Therefore, we can mine coal; that is, take it from under the earth, less expensively. A second advantage of coal is that there is more of it than there is of the other two fuels.

There are four kinds of coal in the world. These are lignite, subbituminous coal, bituminous coal and anthracite. Lignite gives little heat and contains about 75% elementary carbon. Subbituminous coal contains less moisture than lignite and produces (gives out) more heat. Bituminous coal contains less than 86% carbon. Anthracite is the coal with the highest carbon content (86% or more) and gives the most heat. Anthracite also burns slowly and thus maintains - keeps - a uniform and constant fire. These properties of anthracite make it the most preferable kind of coal, especially for domestic uses, in other words, for uses in our homes. One disadvantage of anthracite, however, is that there is less of it than there is of the other kinds of coal.

We can use coal directly in domestic fires, to smelt iron (Fe) in turbines, or to produce steam in steam engines. Our coal supply is large. There is still lots of coal under the ground. We'll probably have enough of it for about 400 years.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. What does 'it' refer to in "Therefore, we can mine coal; that is, take it from under the earth, less expensively"?

2. What does 'the other two fuels' refer to in "A second advantage of coal is that there is more of it than there is of the other two fuels"?

3. What does 'it' refer to in "These properties of anthracite make it the most preferable kind of coal, especially for domestic uses"?

4. What does 'the other kinds of coal' refer to in "One disadvantage of anthracite, however, is that there is less of it than there is of the other kinds of coal"?


*B*

5. What does 'to mine coal' mean in "Therefore, we can mine coal; that is, take it from under the earth, less expensively"?

6. What does 'to produce' mean in "Subbituminous coal contains less moisture than lignite and produces (gives out) more heat"?

7. What does 'to maintain' mean in "Anthracite also burns slowly and thus maintains - keeps - a uniform and constant fire"?

8. What does 'for domestic uses' mean in "These properties of anthracite make it the most preferable kind of coal, especially for domestic uses, in other words, for uses in our homes"?


*C*

9. Coal is obtained from under the ground.

   - True

   - False

10. People on Earth have been using coal for less than 3,000 years.

   - True

   - False

11. The English were the first Europeans who used coal.

   - True

   - False

12. Petroleum is a more important fuel than natural gas.

   - True

   - False

13. If coal contains more carbon, it will give out less heat.

   - True

   - False

14. Lignite contains moisture.

   - True

   - False


*D*

15. After the ____, the use of coal increased greatly during the 18th and 19th centuries.

16. Like petroleum and natural gas, coal is a kind of ____.

17. Subbituminous coal produces more heat than ____, but less than ____.

18. We prefer to use anthracite because it ____ and in this way it ____.

19. We use coal in steam engines to ____.

20. Today's coal supplies will last for ____.


*E*

21. Why can we mine coal less expensively than the other types of fuel?

22. What is a disadvantage of anthracite?


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Inference]* What can be inferred from the passage about the relationship between carbon content and heat in coal?

   - A) Coal with high moisture produces the highest amount of heat.

   - B) Lignite is the best coal for home heating because it burns slowly.

   - C) Coal with higher carbon content generally produces more heat.

   - D) Bituminous coal has a higher carbon percentage than anthracite.

2. *[Author's Purpose]* What is the main purpose of the second paragraph?

   - A) To classify the four types of coal and compare their properties

   - B) To argue that coal should replace petroleum and natural gas

   - C) To explain the chemical process of smelting copper and iron

   - D) To warn readers that world coal supplies will end within decades

3. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In the second paragraph, the word 'uniform' is closest in meaning to:

   - A) extremely hot

   - B) even and steady

   - C) cheap to produce

   - D) difficult to light


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Summary: Coal and Its Varieties** (80-100 words): Summarize the passage by describing coal's history, its advantages over other fuels, the main types of coal, and its current uses.

- **Task 2 — The Future of Coal as an Energy Source** (150-180 words): Coal is still abundant and inexpensive to mine compared to many other fuels. Do you think countries should continue relying heavily on coal, or should they move toward cleaner energy sources? Explain your view with reasons.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **mine** (*maden çıkarmak, kazmak*): Workers mine coal from deep underground tunnels.

- **leading** (*önde gelen, başlıca*): Coal was the leading fuel during the Industrial Revolution.

- **maintain** (*sürdürmek, korumak*): Thick curtains help maintain a comfortable temperature in the room.

- **domestic** (*evsel, ev içi kullanıma yönelik*): Solar panels are mainly used for domestic electricity needs.

- **supply** (*kaynak, stok, arz*): The region has a large supply of fresh water.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Kömürün tarihçesini ve diğer yakıtlara göre avantajlarını özetledim mi?

- [ ] Dört kömür türü arasındaki temel farkları belirttim mi?

- [ ] Neden-sonuç ve karşılaştırma bağlaçlarını doğru kullandım mı?

- [ ] Yazım ve noktalama kurallarını kontrol ettim mi?

## Passage 72: Cars of the Future

- **ID:** `raw1_072` | **Seviye:** `A2` | **Konu:** Future Transportation | **Sayfa:** 93, 94
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_072_cars_of_the_future.mp3` (*AU · en-AU-NatashaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. Future cars will be designed primarily to avoid causing ____. *(İpucu: noun phrase)*

2. Because tomorrow's cars cannot move on ordinary streets, they will require special ____. *(İpucu: noun phrase)*

3. When computers replace human drivers, there will be ____ on the road. *(İpucu: noun phrase)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. What is the main reason car designs will change in the future?

   - A) To make cars travel faster on highways

   - B) To allow people to drive on older city streets

   - C) To protect the environment and stop air pollution

   - D) To reduce the cost of building new roads

2. What will a passenger need to do when traveling in a computer-driven car?

   - A) Steer the car manually during heavy traffic

   - B) Enter the car and tell the computer their destination

   - C) Connect cables to power strips along the road

   - D) Program the computer before every short trip

3. How will future cars get power during long journeys?

   - A) From special power strips built into the road

   - B) From solar panels placed on the car roof

   - C) From petrol stations along highways

   - D) From batteries carried inside the trunk


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Future cars will not be able to drive on ordinary roads.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Car models in the future will look almost identical to the cars we drive today.


### 2. Reading

What kind of cars will we be driving in the year 2015? Rather different ones from those that we know today. The next twenty years will bring greater change in car models than the past fifty years. Tomorrow's cars will not look like those of today. The most important cause that will lead to a change in the design of cars will be environmental. That is, tomorrow's cars will be designed not to cause air pollution. They will be electrically powered; in other words, they will run on electricity entirely and therefore, be environmentally clean. Besides the problem of pollution, there is also the problem of heavy traffic and traffic accidents today. The last two may also be avoided if computers drive the cars instead of drivers. As a passenger, all you will have to do will be to get in the car and say where you want to go. The computer will do the rest and take you there. This, however, will require the construction of special intelligent roads, as tomorrow's models won't be able to move on ordinary roads. These roads will contain special strips that can supply (provide) electrical power to the vehicles as they drive along them. The special equipment in cars will pick up the necessary fuel during long journeys from a power source which will exist in the road. As computers - not drivers - will provide safe driving, there will be fewer accidents; or, maybe, there won't be any accidents at all.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. What does 'ones' refer to in "What kind of cars will we be driving in the year 2015? Rather different ones from those that we know today"?

2. What does 'They' refer to in "That is, tomorrow's cars will be designed not to cause air pollution. They will be electrically powered..."?

3. What does 'These roads' refer to in "This, however, will require the construction of special intelligent roads, as tomorrow's models won't be able to move on ordinary roads. These roads will contain special strips..."?

4. What does 'they' refer to in "These roads will contain special strips that can supply (provide) electrical power to the vehicles as they drive along them"?


*B*

5. Unless there are special roads, we won't be able to use tomorrow's car models.

   - True

   - False

6. Car models will remain the same in the next twenty years.

   - True

   - False

7. Today's cars cause basically two problems.

   - True

   - False


*C*

8. Tomorrow's cars will

   - a) be similar to today's cars

   - b) cause more environmental problems

   - c) run on electricity

   - d) lead to traffic problems

9. The computer will be an important part of tomorrow's cars because it will

   - a) supply electrical power

   - b) drive the car on ordinary roads

   - c) pick up the fuel from the special equipment

   - d) do the driver's job


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Detail]* According to the passage, what three problems do today's cars cause?

   - A) The high cost of fuel and road repairs

   - B) Air pollution, heavy traffic, and traffic accidents

   - C) Lack of parking spaces in big cities

   - D) Slow driving speeds on long journeys

2. *[Inference]* When was this passage most likely written?

   - A) After the year 2015

   - B) About fifty years in the future

   - C) During the early 1950s

   - D) Around twenty years before 2015

3. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In the passage, the word 'avoided' is closest in meaning to:

   - A) prevented

   - B) increased

   - C) studied

   - D) repaired


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Guided Summary: Future Cars** (60-80 words): Write a short summary explaining how future cars will work and what problems they will solve.

- **Task 2 — Traveling in a Self-Driving Car** (80-110 words): Would you like to travel in a self-driving electric car like the ones described in the passage? Why or why not? Write a paragraph giving your opinion.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **air pollution** (*hava kirliliği*): Factories must reduce air pollution in big cities.

- **run on electricity** (*elektrikle çalışmak*): Many new buses run on electricity instead of petrol.

- **avoid** (*kaçınmak, önlemek*): Careful driving helps drivers avoid accidents.

- **construction** (*inşa, yapım*): The construction of the new bridge will finish next year.

- **supply** (*sağlamak, temin etmek*): Solar panels supply power to the entire building.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Geleceğin arabalarının çevre ve trafik sorunlarını nasıl çözeceğini özetledim mi?

- [ ] Akıllı yolların ve bilgisayarların rolünden bahsettim mi?

- [ ] Gelecek zaman (will / won't) yapısını doğru kullandım mı?

- [ ] Kelime sayısı aralığına sadık kaldım mı?

## Passage 73: Using Electricity

- **ID:** `raw1_073` | **Seviye:** `A2` | **Konu:** Electricity & Everyday Technology | **Sayfa:** 94, 95
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_073_using_electricity.mp3` (*UK · en-GB-SoniaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. At home, people use electricity for heating electric fires, irons, and ____. *(İpucu: noun (plural))*

2. Vehicles such as subway trains, trolley buses, and electric locomotives have motors that run on ____. *(İpucu: noun)*

3. Doctors use X-rays to see inside the human body and give a more ____ diagnosis of an illness. *(İpucu: adjective)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. Which of the following uses shaft power to run an electric motor?

   - A) Toasters

   - B) Electric fires

   - C) Washing machines

   - D) Special medical lamps

2. What are ultraviolet and infrared rays used for according to the passage?

   - A) Heating homes

   - B) Medical treatments

   - C) Running subway trains

   - D) Powering factory machines

3. Why are X-rays described as one of the most valuable uses of electricity?

   - A) They replace the need for hospitals.

   - B) They heat the human body during surgery.

   - C) They power electric motors in ambulances.

   - D) They allow doctors to see inside the human body.


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Electricity is only needed inside people's homes.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Producing X-rays is the most expensive medical use of electricity.


### 2. Reading

Our homes are full of devices - machines - that run on electricity, but we don't use electricity in our homes only. We need it in schools, hospitals, banks, factories, etc. Electricity brings us health and comfort. For example, at home we use it for heating electric fires, irons and toasters.

We also use electricity to produce shaft power, i.e., power that we use to run electric motors. For example, washing machines, refrigerators and mixers use this kind of power. Also vehicles such as subway trains, trolley buses and electric locomotives have motors that run on electricity.

There are also some very special lamps that produce ultraviolet rays and infrared rays. We use both rays in medical treatments. One of the most valuable and important uses of electricity is in producing X-rays. When we look at an X-ray, we can see the inside of the human body, and in this way the physicians, or doctors, can give a more accurate; that is, correct, diagnosis of an illness. In other words, when a doctor looks at an X-ray, he can tell you what kind of an illness you have.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. What does 'it' refer to in "We need it in schools, hospitals, banks, factories, etc."?

2. What does 'this kind of power' refer to in "For example, washing machines, refrigerators and mixers use this kind of power."?

3. What does 'both rays' refer to in "We use both rays in medical treatments."?

4. What does 'he' refer to in "In other words, when a doctor looks at an X-ray, he can tell you what kind of an illness you have."?


*B*

5. What does 'devices' mean in "Our homes are full of devices - machines - that run on electricity"? Identify the meaning and the context clue.

6. What does 'physicians' mean in "the physicians, or doctors, can give a more accurate; that is, correct, diagnosis"? Identify the meaning and the context clue.

7. What does 'accurate' mean in "can give a more accurate; that is, correct, diagnosis of an illness"? Identify the meaning and the context clue.


*C*

8. Which devices use electricity to produce heat? (Give two examples.)

9. Which vehicles use shaft power? (Give two examples.)


*D*

10. Complete the sentence: A physician can look at an X-ray and diagnose an illness because an X-ray shows ____.

11. Complete the sentence: ____ and ____ are devices which we use at our homes.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Main Idea]* What is the main idea of the passage?

   - A) How electricity was first discovered in hospitals

   - B) Various ways electricity is used in daily life, transport, and medicine

   - C) Why X-rays are more dangerous than infrared rays

   - D) The differences between subway trains and trolley buses

2. *[Vocabulary in Context]* As used in the final paragraph, the word 'diagnosis' is closest in meaning to:

   - A) Identification of the nature of an illness

   - B) A machine that takes photographs of bones

   - C) A type of electrical treatment for muscles

   - D) A medicine prescribed by a hospital

3. *[Inference]* Which of the following can be inferred from the passage?

   - A) Electric fires and toasters use shaft power to generate heat.

   - B) Infrared lamps are mainly used in schools and banks.

   - C) Both household appliances and certain vehicles rely on electric motors.

   - D) Doctors prefer ultraviolet rays over X-rays for seeing inside the body.


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Guided Summary: Uses of Electricity** (65-85 words): Summarize the main uses of electricity described in the passage in your own words. Mention home, transportation, and medical uses.

- **Task 2 — Opinion Paragraph: The Most Important Use of Electricity** (80-110 words): Which use of electricity from the passage do you think is the most important in modern life? Write a short paragraph explaining your reasons.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **device** (*cihaz, alet*): Modern kitchens have many useful electrical devices.

- **run on electricity** (*elektrikle calismak*): Public transport in big cities often runs on electricity.

- **medical treatment** (*tibbi tedavi*): The hospital offers several new medical treatments for skin problems.

- **physician** (*hekim, doktor*): The physician checked the patient's X-ray carefully.

- **diagnosis** (*teshis, tani*): Early diagnosis helps doctors treat an illness faster.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Metindeki temel elektrik kullanim alanlarinin (ev, ulasim, tip) ucunu de kisaca ozetledim.

- [ ] Baglac ve gecis ifadelerini ('for example', 'also', 'in addition') dogru kullandim.

- [ ] Cumlelerimi A2 seviyesine uygun, acik ve dilbilgisi acisindan dogru kurdum.

- [ ] Hedef kelime sayisi sinirina uydum ve yazim yanlislarini kontrol ettim.

## Passage 74: Polymer Batteries for Electric Vehicles

- **ID:** `raw1_074` | **Seviye:** `B1` | **Konu:** Electric Vehicles & Batteries | **Sayfa:** 96, 97
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_074_polymer_batteries_for_electric_v.mp3` (*US · en-US-GuyNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. One reason for developing electric cars is that they will not cause ____. *(İpucu: noun phrase)*

2. The conductive polymer battery is three times stronger than an ordinary car battery and weighs less than ____. *(İpucu: number and unit)*

3. Because a polymer battery can be ____ and even kill people, it must be insulated well. *(İpucu: adjective)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. Why can't the auto industry yet sell electric passenger cars using lead-acid batteries?

   - A) They are made of conductive plastic.

   - B) They are too heavy, too expensive, and lack enough power.

   - C) They cannot be insulated safely.

   - D) They are only made for subway trains and buses.

2. What makes the conductive polymer battery easy to fit into a car?

   - A) It does not need to be insulated.

   - B) It uses traditional fuel when driving long distances.

   - C) It can be formed into any shape and placed anywhere in the car.

   - D) It never needs to be tested by researchers.

3. Why is the new conductive polymer battery described as cheap?

   - A) The polymers used to make it are inexpensive.

   - B) It weighs more than ordinary batteries.

   - C) It is made from recycled lead-acid batteries.

   - D) It requires less insulation than plastic.


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Some trucks and vans already run on electricity.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — The conductive polymer battery is made entirely of heavy metal.


### 2. Reading

There is an interest in developing electric cars for two reasons. One reason is that there is only a certain amount of fuel in the world and it will eventually finish. Another is that electric cars will not cause air pollution; i.e. the air will be clean. Why aren't there electric cars on the roads then?

Although there are trucks, vans and other vehicles which run on electricity, the auto industry cannot yet produce electric passenger cars in order to sell on the market. This is because of the batteries in the cars today. These are lead-acid batteries and they do not provide the necessary power for an electric car. In other words, the car cannot go a long distance with such a battery. Besides, you must replace it in time.

Most engineers believe that the lead-acid battery is too heavy and too expensive. Therefore, scientists have developed another type of battery. This development may now be the first important step in the mass production of electric automobiles. The new type is a conductive polymer battery. It is made of a material which is similar to plastic. However, it has a metallic appearance and the ability to conduct electricity. This new plastic battery is three times stronger than the ordinary car battery and weighs less than 5 kilograms. In addition, car builders can form it into any shape. This means you can place the battery anywhere in the car; for example, under the roof, behind the door panels or even under the seat. Besides its capacity to take various shapes, it is also cheap because of the polymers used.

Several types of conductive polymer batteries are ready to be tested. One disadvantage is that the polymer battery can be toxic - it can even kill people; therefore, it must be insulated well.

Many researchers agree that it is important to solve the energy problem; so the conductive polymer battery may be an important first step in electric vehicle development. They believe that such vehicles will replace the ones which we use today.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. Based on "Although there are trucks, vans and other vehicles which run on electricity", complete the sentence: A van is ____.

2. Based on "One disadvantage is that the polymer battery can be toxic - it can even kill people", complete the sentence: A toxic material can ____.


*B*

3. What does 'it' refer to in "One reason is that there is only a certain amount of fuel in the world and it will eventually finish."?

4. What does 'Another' refer to in "Another is that electric cars will not cause air pollution"?

5. What does 'it' refer to in "It is made of a material which is similar to plastic. However, it has a metallic appearance and the ability to conduct electricity."?

6. What does 'This' refer to in "In addition, car builders can form it into any shape. This means you can place the battery anywhere in the car"?

7. What does 'ones' refer to in "They believe that such vehicles will replace the ones which we use today."?


*C*

8. Because of lead-acid batteries, ____.

   - a) electric vehicles can carry passengers

   - b) the auto industry cannot produce electric batteries

   - c) electric passenger cars are not common today

   - d) there are electric trucks, vans and other vehicles

9. Which of the following is a property of a conductive polymer battery?

   - a) It is made of a kind of conductive plastic.

   - b) Although it's made of metal, it looks like plastic.

   - c) It shows strong resistance to heavy weights.

   - d) It lacks the ability to conduct electricity.


*D*

10. Write three of the disadvantages of a lead-acid battery.

11. Write three of the advantages of a conductive polymer battery.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Author's Purpose]* What is the primary purpose of the passage?

   - A) How to repair lead-acid batteries in trucks and vans

   - B) Why air pollution cannot be solved by electric vehicles

   - C) Why electric passenger cars have been delayed and how polymer batteries may solve the problem

   - D) The history of plastic manufacturing in the auto industry

2. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In paragraph 4, the word 'insulated' most likely means:

   - A) Made heavier with metal plates

   - B) Heated before installation

   - C) Sold cheaply on the market

   - D) Covered with protective material to prevent danger

3. *[Inference]* What can be inferred from the passage about conductive polymer batteries?

   - A) Conductive polymer batteries are not yet widely used in commercial passenger cars.

   - B) Lead-acid batteries are safer and stronger than conductive polymer batteries.

   - C) Electric passenger cars have already replaced all fuel-powered vehicles.

   - D) Polymer batteries can only be placed under the car's seat.


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Summary & Paraphrase: From Lead-Acid to Polymer Batteries** (80-100 words): Paraphrase the main problem with traditional car batteries and explain how conductive polymer batteries aim to solve it, including their main drawback.

- **Task 2 — Opinion Mini-Essay: The Future of Electric Vehicles** (120-160 words): Do you think governments should invest heavily in replacing fuel-powered cars with electric vehicles? Discuss the benefits and potential challenges in a short essay.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **air pollution** (*hava kirliligi*): Burning fossil fuels causes serious air pollution in large cities.

- **mass production** (*seri uretim*): Mass production made automobiles affordable for ordinary families.

- **conduct electricity** (*elektrigi iletmek*): Copper has a strong ability to conduct electricity.

- **toxic** (*zehirli, toksik*): Certain industrial chemicals are toxic and must be handled carefully.

- **insulate** (*yalitmak, izole etmek*): Electrical wires must be insulated to protect users from shocks.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Kursun-asit ve iletken polimer piller arasindaki temel farklari kendi cumlelerimle ozetledim.

- [ ] Gorusu destekleyen en az iki somut neden (cevre, yakit tukenmesi, maliyet veya guvenlik) sundum.

- [ ] Karsilastirma ve neden-sonuc baglaclarini ('however', 'therefore', 'in addition') dogru kullandim.

- [ ] Kelime sinirina uydum ve dilbilgisi hatalarini gozden gecirdim.
