## Passage 75: Effects of Cigarette Smoking

- **ID:** `raw1_075` | **Seviye:** `B1` | **Konu:** Health & Smoking Risks | **Sayfa:** 98, 99
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_075_effects_of_cigarette_smoking.mp3` (*US · en-US-AriaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. Nothing can completely eliminate the tar without changing the ____ of the cigarette completely. *(İpucu: noun)*

2. An ____ brain, meaning a brain without enough oxygen, begins to die. *(İpucu: compound adjective)*

3. Smokers are forced to keep smoking because of their dependence on a strong stimulant called ____. *(İpucu: noun)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. Which disease is specifically linked in the passage to inhaling cigarette smoke?

   - A) Lung cancer

   - B) Tongue cancer

   - C) Brain damage

   - D) Throat cancer

2. Why does the passage say the effects of carbon monoxide are perhaps more serious?

   - A) It completely changes the taste of the cigarette.

   - B) It acts as a calming agent on the nerves.

   - C) It lowers oxygen levels in the blood and brain and can harm others too.

   - D) It is only harmful to people who inhale deeply.

3. Why do smokers mistakenly believe that smoking calms their nerves?

   - A) Nicotine slows down the nervous system.

   - B) Smoking increases oxygen flow to the brain.

   - C) Filters remove the stimulating chemicals from smoke.

   - D) Satisfying their dependence creates a psychological feeling of relief.


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Special filters can remove all the tar from a cigarette without affecting its taste.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Coffee and tea cause the same kind of permanent brain damage as carbon monoxide.


### 2. Reading

There are three harmful substances in cigarettes: tar, carbon monoxide and nicotine. Cigarette smokers who inhale the smoke, or breathe it in, can develop lung cancer. Other smokers who only take the smoke into their mouths can suffer from throat, tongue and larynx cancers. The substance which causes cancer is the tar in cigarettes. Cigarette manufacturers have tried to develop low tar brands of cigarettes to reduce the dangers. Certain forms of filters in addition to the cigarette's own filter can also help. However, nothing can completely eliminate, i.e., remove, the tar without changing the taste of the cigarette completely.

The effects of carbon monoxide are perhaps more serious because it can cause permanent damage - damage which lasts for a very long time - on others besides the smokers themselves. Increased carbon monoxide intake automatically means reduced oxygen intake, and consequently a lower oxygen content in the blood and the brain. An oxygen-starved brain (a brain without enough oxygen) begins to die.

It is true that cigarette smokers need cigarettes. Of course they do not actively choose to harm themselves and others, but they are forced to do so because of their dependence on the strong and fast-acting stimulant called nicotine. In other words, they can't do without it. People who smoke frequently claim that smoking makes them feel calm and soothes their nerves, but this is a physiological impossibility, not a real effect at all. Actually it appears to be the psychological consequence of satisfying a need because cigarettes, like coffee and tea, are arousing agents and they make people more nervous, not less.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. Guess the meaning of 'inhale' from the passage and complete the sentence: When you inhale something, you ____.

2. Guess the meaning of 'eliminate' from the passage and complete the sentence: Cigarette manufacturers try to eliminate the tar. That is, they try to ____.

3. Guess the meaning of 'permanent' from the passage and complete the sentence: If something is permanent, it ____.

4. Guess the meaning of 'oxygen-starved' from the passage and complete the sentence: If the brain is oxygen-starved, it doesn't ____.

5. Guess the meaning of 'dependence' from the passage and complete the sentence: If you have dependence on something, you ____.

6. Guess the meaning of 'nicotine' from the passage and complete the sentence: Nicotine is a ____.


*B*

7. What does 'they' refer to in "Of course they do not actively choose to harm themselves and others"?

8. What does 'they' refer to in "because cigarettes, like coffee and tea, are arousing agents and they make people more nervous, not less."?


*C*

9. What kind of smokers can have throat cancer?

10. What can reduce the tar in cigarettes?

11. Why will a person probably feel more nervous when he smokes?

   - C) Why coffee and tea are healthier than cigarettes

   - D) The history of cigarette filter design

2. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In the last sentence, the phrase 'arousing agents' refers to substances that are:

   - A) Causing deep sleep

   - B) Completely harmless

   - C) Stimulating or increasing nervous activity

   - D) Relieving physical pain

3. *[Detail]* Which statement is supported by the passage?

   - A) People near smokers can suffer long-term harm from carbon monoxide.

   - B) Low-tar cigarettes eliminate the risk of nicotine addiction.

   - C) Only smokers who inhale deeply suffer any health risks from cigarettes.

   - D) Nicotine causes lung cancer more directly than tar does.


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Summary: The Three Harmful Substances in Cigarettes** (70-95 words): Summarize the main health effects of tar, carbon monoxide, and nicotine as explained in the passage.

- **Task 2 — Opinion Mini-Essay: Banning Smoking in Public Places** (120-160 words): Should smoking be banned in all public places? Write a short opinion essay supporting your view using ideas from the passage and your own reasons.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **eliminate** (*ortadan kaldirmak, gidermek*): New laws aim to eliminate smoking in indoor areas.

- **dependence** (*bagimlilik*): Nicotine dependence makes it very hard to quit smoking.

## Passage 76: Microscopic Organisms

- **ID:** `raw1_076` | **Seviye:** `B1` | **Konu:** Microbiology & Cell Structure | **Sayfa:** 99, 100, 101
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_076_microscopic_organisms.mp3` (*UK · en-GB-RyanNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. In the seventeenth century, Antonie van Leeuwenhoek called the tiny organisms he saw with a microscope ____. *(İpucu: plural noun)*

2. Plant-like protists can make their own food because they have ____. *(İpucu: noun)*

3. Scientists can see a virus only with the ____. *(İpucu: noun phrase)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. According to the passage, what is true about a bacterium's cell?

   - A) It has multiple nuclei and no cell wall.

   - B) It contains chlorophyll so it can make its own food.

   - C) It has a cell membrane and a cell wall, but no nucleus.

   - D) It is surrounded by a protein cover instead of a membrane.

2. Why do scientists have difficulty classifying viruses as living or non-living?

   - A) Because they move too quickly under a microscope

   - B) Because they are inactive outside a cell and reproduce only inside one

   - C) Because they change from bacteria into protists over time

   - D) Because they have chlorophyll like plants but eat other organisms like animals

3. Which two kingdoms were added to help classify microscopic organisms?

   - A) Animalcules and protozoa

   - B) Plants and viruses

   - C) Cytoplasm and bacteria

   - D) Protists and monera


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Both animal-like and plant-like protists serve as food for other aquatic plants and animals.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — A virus is a complete single cell that contains both cytoplasm and a nucleus.


### 2. Reading

In the seventeenth century, Antonie van Leeuwenhoek was the first person to see tiny organisms with a microscope. He called them animalcules. Later, scientists in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries named these animalcules bacteria and protozoa. This was the beginning of the sciences of Bacteriology and Protozoology, the studies of microscopic organisms. Bacteriologists and protozoologists have studied these organisms for many years, but they find it difficult to classify them.

Scientists cannot classify some of these microscopic organisms in the plant or animal kingdom, so they put them into another kingdom, protists. Some protists are like animals. They do not have chlorophyll, and they cannot make their own food. These protists get their food from other organisms.

Other protists are like plants. They have chlorophyll, and they can make their own food. They usually live in water. Both animal and plant-like protists provide food for other plants and animals that live in the water. Many protists are single-celled organisms. They have only one cell. Others, however, are multicelled. They have many cells. Because these organisms are neither plants nor animals, scientists put them in another kingdom.

Bacteria are also difficult to classify in the plant or animal kingdoms. They have only one cell, but the cell does not have a nucleus. It contains only a cell membrane and a cell wall. Bacteria cannot make their own food. They must get the food from other organisms. Some bacteriologists classify bacteria separately in the monera kingdom.

Another microscopic organism is the virus. It is much smaller than protists or bacteria. Scientists can see it only with the electron microscope. A virus is not a cell. It is simpler than a cell. It does not have a cytoplasm or a nucleus. It has a cover of protein, and inside the protein, there is reproductive material. This reproductive material helps the virus reproduce. It makes more viruses.

The virus reproduces only when it is inside another cell. When it enters another cell, it begins to reproduce. It makes more and more viruses inside the cell until the cell breaks open and the viruses go into other cells. For this reason, scientists have difficulty classifying it as living or non-living. Outside another cell, the virus is inactive. It does not become active and reproduce until it enters another cell.

Although we cannot see them, microscopic organisms are everywhere. They are an important part of life on the earth. It is difficult to classify these organisms, because they are different from other plants and animals. Some of them have chlorophyll like plants, and others do not. Some of them are not complete cells. Bacteria do not have a nucleus, and viruses do not have cytoplasm. To help classify microscopic organisms, some bacteriologists have added two more kingdoms: the protists and the monera.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. Which of the following is the main idea of this article?

   - a) It is difficult to classify microscopic organisms.

   - b) Microscopic organisms are very small.

   - c) Viruses cause many dangerous diseases which doctors cannot cure.

   - d) Some protists are like animals and some are like plants.

2. Mark three reasons why microscopic organisms are difficult to classify.

   - a) Some are not like either plants or animals.

   - b) Some are plants because they have chlorophyll.

   - c) Some are not complete cells.

   - d) Some are animals and cannot make their own food by photosynthesis.

   - e) Some have different shapes.

   - f) Scientists are not sure if some are living or non-living.


*B*

3. Guess the meaning of 'Bacteriology and Protozoology' in "This was the beginning of the sciences of Bacteriology and Protozoology, the studies of microscopic organisms."

4. Guess the meaning of 'single-celled' in "Many protists are single-celled organisms. They have only one cell."

5. Guess the meaning of 'reproduce' in "This reproductive material helps the virus reproduce. It makes more viruses."

6. Guess the meaning of 'inactive' in "Outside another cell, the virus is inactive. It does not become active and reproduce until it enters another cell."


*C*

7. What does 'them' refer to in "Bacteriologists and protozoologists have studied these organisms for many years, but they find it difficult to classify them."?

8. What does 'they' refer to in "Scientists cannot classify some of these microscopic organisms in the plant or animal kingdom, so they put them into another kingdom, protists."?

9. What does 'They' refer to in "Some protists are like animals. They do not have chlorophyll, and they cannot make their own food."?

10. Complete the sentence: 'Others' in "Many protists are single-celled organisms. They have only one cell. Others, however, are multicelled." refers to other _____.

11. What does 'It' refer to in "They have only one cell, but the cell does not have a nucleus. It contains only a cell membrane and a cell wall."?


*D*

12. How many kingdoms of organisms does the writer mention? Write their names.

13. What is the difference between the animal-like protists and the plant-like protists?

14. What is the similarity between animal-like protists and plant-like protists?

15. In what ways are bacteria and viruses different? Give two differences.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Inference]* What can be inferred about viruses from the way they reproduce?

   - A) Viruses can survive independently by making their own food.

   - B) Viruses destroy other cells mainly to obtain chlorophyll.

   - C) Viruses depend completely on host cells in order to multiply.

   - D) Viruses become larger cells once they enter another organism.

2. *[Author's Purpose]* What is the author's main purpose in writing this passage?

   - A) To prove that Antonie van Leeuwenhoek made errors in his research

   - B) To explain why microscopic organisms are hard to group into existing kingdoms

   - C) To warn readers about the dangers of viral infections in water

   - D) To compare the power of light microscopes and electron microscopes

3. *[Detail]* Which of the following is true about bacteria according to the passage?

   - A) They lack a nucleus and obtain food from other organisms.

   - B) They are multicelled and usually live in water.

   - C) They contain cytoplasm and reproduce inside protein covers.

   - D) They have chlorophyll and are placed in the protist kingdom.


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Summary: Classifying Microscopic Organisms** (90-110 words): Summarize in your own words why scientists find it difficult to classify microscopic organisms (protists, bacteria, and viruses) and how they have addressed this challenge.

- **Task 2 — Opinion Paragraph: Why Microbiology Matters in Daily Life** (110-140 words): Do you think it is important for ordinary people—not only scientists—to learn about microscopic organisms such as bacteria and viruses? Write an opinion paragraph explaining your reasons with examples.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **classify** (*sınıflandırmak*): Scientists classify living things into different kingdoms based on their cell structure.

- **nucleus** (*çekirdek (hücre çekirdeği)*): Unlike animal cells, a bacterial cell does not contain a nucleus.

- **reproduce** (*üremek, çoğalmak*): Most viruses need a living host cell in order to reproduce quickly.

- **inactive** (*etkisiz, hareketsiz, pasif*): The volcano has been inactive for several decades.

- **chlorophyll** (*klorofil*): Green plants use chlorophyll to absorb sunlight and produce their own food.

- **microscopic organism** (*mikroskobik organizma*): Microscopic organisms are an essential part of aquatic ecosystems.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Metindeki üç ana mikroskobik organizma türünü (protistler, bakteriler ve virüsler) doğru şekilde özetledim mi?

- [ ] Sınıflandırmanın neden zor olduğunu kendi cümlelerimle net bir biçimde açıkladım mı?

- [ ] Paragraf boyunca uygun bağlaçlar (whereas, however, because, therefore vb.) kullandım mı?

- [ ] Yazım, noktalama ve özne-yüklem uyumu hatalarını kontrol ettim mi?

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Famous singer Linda Ronstadt once roller-skated to a dinner party in Los Angeles.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Renting roller skates in New York's Central Park costs five dollars an hour.


### 2. Reading

Every weekday morning mechanical engineer John Buchan, 23, puts on a suit and tie and roller-skates seven miles across San Francisco to his office. He is not alone. In Los Angeles, the famous singer Linda Ronstadt once skated to a dinner party. In New York's Central Park, people wait in line to rent skates on weekends. And on Tuesday nights city skaters join in "Nightskates", a two-hour trip through the park.

The new interest in roller-skating is the result of a new kind of skate which has polyurethane wheels. These wheels make skating very easy. However, the new skates are expensive, so many people prefer to rent them.

As well as providing transportation and fun, roller-skating is also becoming an organised sport. Today the U.S. Amateur Confederation of Roller Skating has about 40,000 members. National teams of roller skaters will compete in next year's American Games and skaters hope that skating is going to be a part of the Olympic Games in the near future.

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. In 1877, astronomers looked through improved telescopes and saw two very small objects that turned out to be two ____ in orbit around Mars. *(İpucu: noun (plural))*

2. The largest volcano discovered by Mariner 9 on Mars is called ____. *(İpucu: proper noun)*

3. Viking 1 and Viking 2 discovered the gases ____ and xenon in the Martian atmosphere. *(İpucu: noun (gas))*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. How long does it take Mars to complete one turn around itself?

   - A) 24 hours, 37 minutes, and 27 seconds

   - B) 23 hours, 56 minutes, and 4 seconds

   - C) 11 months and 20 days

   - D) 25 hours and 50 minutes

2. How many photographs did Mariner 4 send back to Earth in 1965?

   - A) Two

   - B) Eleven

   - C) Twenty-two

   - D) Five hundred

3. How did Viking 1 and Viking 2 deal with damaged parts while on Mars?

   - A) Astronomers repaired them remotely from Florida

   - B) They were able to repair the damage themselves

   - C) Mariner 9 assisted them from orbit around Mars

   - D) They returned to Earth after eleven months


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Mariner 4 landed on Mars in 1965 before sending photographs to Earth.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Viking 2 was heavier and carried more scientific instruments than Viking 1.


### 2. Reading

(by Dr. Ken Grady, 1980)

People have always been quite excited about details on Mars because Earth and Mars have many characteristics in common. Just like Earth, Mars turns around itself. It takes Mars twenty-four hours, thirty-seven minutes and twenty-seven seconds to complete one turn, so the day on Mars is a bit longer than ours. Besides, both planets have similar seasons. Because of these similarities, astronomers were quite sure that there was life on Mars.

In 1877, with the help of improved telescopes, astronomers saw two objects around Mars. Even serious astronomers said these two very small objects were spacecraft. In fact, they were two moons in orbit around Mars. In the same year, Schiaparelli, a well-known astronomer of the time, drew a map of Mars. Although this was not the first map of Mars, it created a lot of interest among astronomers. The map showed lakes, seas and forests. It also showed narrow lines. Schiaparelli called these lines canali in Italian. The correct English word for canali is channels, but it came into English as canals (which means man-made waterways). Because of this mistake, people thought that there were living things on Mars and they built these canals to carry water from one place to another.

In 1965 the spacecraft Mariner 4 flew past Mars. It did not land on Mars, but it sent Earth twenty-two photographs. They gave a good idea of the surface of Mars - a place full of craters and high areas of volcanic rock. Then in 1971, Mariner 9 discovered four volcanoes on Mars. They were much larger than the volcanoes here. The largest one, Olympus Mons, was 25 kilometres high and 500 kilometres from side to side.

In 1976, two spacecraft, Viking 1 and Viking 2, landed on the surface of Mars. Radio signals from Earth controlled the two spacecraft. Viking 1 and Viking 2 could feel and control their environment. They could also self-repair. That is, when there was a problem with a part of the spacecraft, they could repair the damage themselves.

Viking 1 left Cape Canaveral, Florida, for Mars on August 20, 1975. It travelled around the sun and it took eleven months to complete its trip of almost 1,000 million kilometres. It was very difficult for Viking 1 to find a safe landing place because of the strong winds and the rough rocky surface of Mars. Finally, on July 20, 1976, it landed safely on Mars. And Viking 2 landed on Mars on September 3, 1976.

The two Vikings sent a lot of new information to Earth. For example, they discovered the existence of the gases krypton and xenon in the Martian atmosphere. (Astronomers already knew about carbon dioxide, water vapour, oxygen, nitrogen and argon.)

Astronomers feel that many more important discoveries about Mars are possible. They expect to find some form of life there in the future. In my opinion, they won't know that for sure until they go to Mars.

*B*

   - B) Mariner 4 photographed artificial waterways across the Martian craters

   - C) The Italian word 'canali' was mistranslated into English as 'canals'

   - D) Two small moons around Mars appeared to carry water between seas

2. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In the context of Viking 1's landing, what does 'rough' mean in the phrase 'the strong winds and the rough rocky surface of Mars'?

   - A) Uneven and rocky

   - B) Smooth and flat

   - C) Soft and muddy

   - D) Warm and grassy

3. *[Inference]* What can be inferred about Dr. Ken Grady's view at the end of the passage?

   - B) He thinks Schiaparelli's map accurately showed forests and lakes

### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Summarizing Early Mars Exploration** (80-100 words): Write a summary explaining how scientists' understanding of Mars developed from the nineteenth century to the Viking missions of 1976.

- **Task 2 — Human vs. Robotic Exploration of Mars** (120-150 words): At the end of the passage, Dr. Grady says scientists will not know for sure whether there is life on Mars until they go there. Do you think we should send human astronauts to Mars, or is it better to explore the planet only with robots and spacecraft? Give reasons for your opinion.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **have ... in common** (*ortak özelliklere sahip olmak*): Earth and Mars have several characteristics in common, such as similar seasons.

- **in orbit** (*yörüngede*): The two moons remain in orbit around Mars throughout the year.

- **volcanic rock** (*volkanik kaya*): Photographs from Mariner 4 showed high areas of volcanic rock on the surface.

- **self-repair** (*kendi kendini onarmak*): Advanced spacecraft are designed to self-repair when small parts are damaged.

- **atmosphere** (*atmosfer*): Scientists studied the gases in the Martian atmosphere using data from Viking 1 and 2.

- **existence** (*varlık, mevcudiyet*): Space missions confirmed the existence of krypton and xenon on Mars.

## Passage 79: Pirates

- **ID:** `raw1_079` | **Seviye:** `B1` | **Konu:** History & Piracy | **Sayfa:** 106, 107, 108
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_079_pirates.mp3` (*UK · en-GB-SoniaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. Pirates existed for about ____ years, from the 16th to the 18th century. *(İpucu: number)*

2. In reality, pirates dressed very badly in ____ and wasted food and money. *(İpucu: plural noun)*

3. The pirate Jean Lafitte became a ____ and helped American forces protect New Orleans. *(İpucu: noun)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. How were pirate groups organized according to the passage?

   - A) They always obeyed the laws of their home governments.

   - B) They refused to make any rules for their ships.

   - C) They elected their own captains and created rules.

   - D) They shared all their gold with Spanish colonies.

2. What does the passage say about the popular image of pirates?

   - A) They were always wealthy and lived very long lives.

   - B) Books and movies created a romantic picture of them as handsome gentlemen.

   - C) They never carried weapons such as swords or pistols.

   - D) They dressed in rags in popular adventure films.

3. Which two famous women pirates are mentioned at the end of the passage?

   - A) Anne Bonney and Mary Read

   - B) Jean Lafitte and Henry Morgan

   - C) Robert Louis Stevenson and Rafael Sabatini

   - D) Blackbeard and Captain Kidd


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Most real pirates lived long and healthy lives.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Spanish ships carrying gold and silver to Europe were often attacked by pirates.


### 2. Reading

Pirates were people who attacked and robbed ships on all oceans of the world. They were sea robbers or bandits and have been called by many other names such as: buccaneers, corsairs, filibusters, freebooters, landrones, picaroons, and rovers.

Pirates existed for about 200 years, from the 16th to the 18th century. They used to attack and capture ships for the valuable cargo, leave their ships to break into homes in coastal towns and carry away valuables, take people to their ships by using force, and organize powerful groups to get large amounts of ransom, which was paid to free a captured person.

Probably most people have romantic ideas about pirates. The movies and some famous books such as Treasure Island by Robert Louis Stevenson and Captain Blood by Rafael Sabatini have helped to create a romantic picture of pirates. The pirate in films and books is a cavalier, a good-looking gentleman, with a beard or moustache, gold earrings and a large hat or turban. He usually has a sword or a sharp dagger in his belt and a pistol or gun in his hand.

In reality, pirates were not romantic at all. Often they were desperate, violent and dangerous people who drank a lot and dressed very badly in rags and wasted food and money. They were people who did not like the situation of their home society and, therefore, rebelled and fought against the government. The police looked for them everywhere because they were outlaws, that is, people who committed crimes and hid from the authorities. Most pirates did not live long.

A kind of democracy often existed among pirate groups. They elected, or chose their own captains and prepared rules and regulations to use when they were doing business.

During the 1600's and 1700's there was a lot of piracy along the American coasts and in the West Indies. The great treasure ships of Spain which carried gold and silver from Spanish colonies to Europe were frequently attacked and the valuables were stolen by pirates. But there were also times when the American government asked for the help of pirates. Many of the American pirates became privateers during the American War for Independence. The pirate, Jean Lafitte, for instance, became a privateer and helped American military forces to protect New Orleans in the War of 1812.

Among the men, some famous pirate names in history are: Barbarossa, Ali Pichinin, Henry Morgan, Captain Kidd, "Black Bart" (Bartholomew Roberts), "Blackbeard" (Edward Teach) and among the women: Anne Bonney and Mary Read.

   - D) To explain why Spain stopped sending ships to Europe

   - B) Pirates who gave up sailing to become police officers

   - C) Former pirates who fought on behalf of a government during wartime

   - D) Captains who were elected without any rules or regulations

   - A) Pirates earned large fortunes and retired peacefully in coastal towns.

   - B) Most pirates came from wealthy families who supported their crimes.

   - D) Many people became pirates due to dissatisfaction with their own society.


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Fiction vs. Reality: The Life of Pirates** (70-90 words): Summarize the difference between the romantic image of pirates and the reality described in the passage in your own words. Mention how pirate groups organized themselves and how governments sometimes used them.

- **Task 2 — Why Piracy Still Fascinates People** (100-130 words): Why do you think stories and movies about pirates are still so popular today, even though real pirates were violent criminals? Give reasons and examples to support your opinion.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **ransom** (*fidye*): The kidnappers demanded a large ransom before releasing the ship's captain.

- **rebel against** (*isyan etmek, baskaldirmak*): Many people rebelled against the unfair taxes imposed by the king.

- **outlaw** (*kanun kacagi, haydut*): The outlaws hid in the mountains so that the authorities could not find them.

- **rags** (*pacavra, eski pusku giysiler*): In reality, the old sailor lived a difficult life and wore worn-out rags.

- **rules and regulations** (*kural ve yonetmelikler*): Every company must follow safety rules and regulations to protect its workers.

- **cargo** (*yuk, kargo*): A large cargo ship carried grain and machinery across the Atlantic Ocean.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

## Passage 80: Geothermal Energy

- **ID:** `raw1_080` | **Seviye:** `B1` | **Konu:** Energy & Earth Science | **Sayfa:** 109, 110
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_080_geothermal_energy.mp3` (*US · en-US-GuyNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. In Southampton, England, engineers found hot water at 1500 metres with a temperature of 60 ____. *(İpucu: temperature unit)*

2. In California, hot water and steam are hot enough to drive electric ____. *(İpucu: plural noun)*

3. In Los Alamos, scientists plan to send ____ down to the broken hot rock. *(İpucu: noun phrase)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. What is described as a normal heat gradient?

   - A) 15 degrees Celsius per kilometre

   - B) 20 degrees Celsius per kilometre

   - C) 25 degrees Celsius per kilometre

   - D) 40 degrees Celsius per kilometre

2. Why do people NOT need to drill for geothermal energy in places like Iceland and New Zealand?

   - A) The water underground is always too cold to use.

   - B) Hot water and steam already come out of the ground naturally.

   - C) Drilling equipment cannot reach deep rocks in those countries.

   - D) There is no hot dry rock anywhere in those regions.

3. How can the hot underground water found in Southampton be used?

   - A) To drive electric generators for all of England

   - B) To cool down hot dry rocks underground

   - C) To export steam to Iceland and Italy

   - D) To heat a thousand houses for thirty or forty years


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Geothermal energy is used to heat most of Reykjavik, the capital of Iceland.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — The speaker believes geothermal energy will become the world's most important energy source.


### 2. Reading

It gets hotter and hotter as you go down towards the centre of the earth. This heat is geothermal energy. In some places the temperature increases as much as 40°C per kilometre, and this is a very good 'heat gradient'. A normal heat gradient is 25°C per kilometre. Often if you make a deep hole in the ground, you will find hot water deep underground. For example, engineers drilled for hot water in Southampton, England. At 1500 metres they found water at 60°C. There is enough hot water there to heat a thousand houses for thirty or forty years.

In some places you do not have to drill to obtain geothermal energy. This is because hot water and steam are already coming out of the ground. The most famous examples of this are in California (USA), New Zealand, Italy and Iceland. In these places the water and steam are very hot. It is hot enough to heat most of the capital of Iceland, Reykjavik, or to drive, i.e. provide the power to work, electric generators in California.

There is not always hot water deep down, but there is often very hot dry rock. Of course, we want to use this heat, but can we get it out? In Los Alamos, USA, scientists are trying to do this. They are drilling down to the hot rock. Then they are going to break the hot rock. They will then send cold water down to the bottom, and it will get hot there. This hot water will return to the top through another hole. The hot water will be useful for making electricity or heating houses.

Geothermal energy will never be an important source of energy. But there are many places where it can be very useful.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A. Reference and Vocabulary.*

1. 1. In the sentence "In these places the water and steam are very hot.", 'these places' refers to

   - a) Iceland and Reykjavik

   - b) California, New Zealand, Italy and Iceland

   - c) Italy and California

   - d) New Zealand, Italy and Iceland

2. 2. In the sentence "They will then send cold water down to the bottom, and it will get hot there.", what does 'it' refer to?

3. 3. In the sentence "Geothermal energy will never be an important source of energy. But there are many places where it can be very useful.", what does 'it' refer to?

4. 4. In the sentence "For example, engineers drilled for hot water in Southampton, England.", 'drilled' means

   - a) found hot water

   - b) increased the temperature

   - c) made holes in the ground

   - d) heated the houses

5. 5. In the phrase "to drive, i.e. provide the power to work, electric generators in California", what does 'drive' mean?


*B. Mark the statements as True (T) or False (F).*

6. 1. 25°C per kilometre is a very good heat gradient.

   - True

   - False

7. 2. There is enough geothermal energy in the world to use instead of oil.

   - True

   - False


*C. Find the sentence in the passage which gives the following information and write it down.*

8. Find the sentence in the passage which gives the following information and write it down: "It is possible to get geothermal energy without drilling in certain places."


*D. Mark the best choice.*

9. 1. In Los Alamos, scientists

   - a) are trying to heat the underground rocks

   - b) want to use the heat of the underground hot dry rock

   - c) cannot drill down to the hot rock

   - d) have found hot water

10. 2. Which of the following is true?

   - a) They make electricity from geothermal energy in California.

   - b) In England, engineers haven't found any hot underground water yet.

   - c) You can always find hot water deep underground.

   - d) In Los Alamos, scientists will send hot water to the bottom.


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Detail]* How do scientists in Los Alamos plan to extract heat from underground dry rock?

   - A) By drilling two holes, breaking the rock, and circulating water through it

   - B) By waiting for natural steam to break through the surface by itself

   - C) By dropping electric generators directly onto the underground rocks

   - D) By pumping oil into the rock to make it burn hotter

2. *[Author's Purpose]* What is the author's attitude toward geothermal energy?

   - A) Extremely enthusiastic and convinced it will replace all fossil fuels

   - B) Completely pessimistic that geothermal heat has any practical value

   - C) Objective and realistic, noting both its practical uses and its limits

   - D) Critical of engineers who spend money drilling in England

3. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In paragraph 2, the word "obtain" is closest in meaning to ____.

   - A) destroy

   - B) get

   - C) waste

   - D) measure


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — How We Obtain Geothermal Energy** (70-95 words): Summarize the three different ways geothermal energy can be obtained according to the passage, and give one example location for each method.

- **Task 2 — Investing in Geothermal Energy** (100-130 words): Do you think countries should invest more money in geothermal energy even though it cannot supply energy to every part of the world? Explain your reasons.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **drill** (*sondaj yapmak, delmek*): Engineers used special machines to drill deep into the ground in search of hot water.

- **obtain** (*elde etmek, saglamak*): Solar panels allow homeowners to obtain electricity directly from sunlight.

- **steam** (*buhar*): High-pressure steam from underground pipes can turn turbines to make electricity.

- **electric generator** (*elektrik jeneratoru*): During a power cut, the hospital used emergency electric generators to keep machines running.

- **temperature increases** (*sicaklik artmak*): The water temperature increases steadily as you go deeper beneath the surface.

- **source of energy** (*enerji kaynagi*): Geothermal heat is a reliable source of energy in volcanic regions like Iceland.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Jeotermal enerjinin nasil elde edildigini uc farkli yontemle (sondajla sicak su bulma, dogal buhar ve kuru sicak kaya) acikladim mi?

- [ ] Pasajdaki ornek yerleri (Southampton, Izlanda, Kaliforniya, Los Alamos) dogru sekilde yazdim mi?

- [ ] Surec anlatiminda sira bildiren ifadeler (first, then, finally vb.) kullandim mi?

- [ ] Cumle kurulumunu, ozne-yuklem uyumunu ve yazim kurallarini kontrol ettim mi?

## Passage 81: Bali

- **ID:** `raw1_081` | **Seviye:** `A2` | **Konu:** Tourism & Culture | **Sayfa:** 111, 112
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_081_bali.mp3` (*US · en-US-AriaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. The people work all day at home, in the fields or in their ____, and make music in the evenings. *(İpucu: plural noun)*

2. Before the ____, the Balinese people did not worry about the outside world. *(İpucu: decade)*

3. Because too many people live on the island now, the Balinese need to buy ____ and other things from abroad. *(İpucu: uncountable noun)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. Why are there fewer temple festivals in Bali nowadays?

   - A) More temples are being built across the island.

   - B) Tourists prefer not to attend temple ceremonies.

   - C) People spend more time making things for tourists than for their temples.

   - D) The authorities have banned traditional festivals.

2. Why are the Balinese authorities trying to control tourism carefully?

   - A) To stop all tourists from visiting the island completely.

   - B) To make sure Bali changes for the better, not for the worse.

   - C) To prevent Balinese artists from selling their work.

   - D) To keep food prices low for local farmers.


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Only a few people in Bali are interested in making art or music.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — The Balinese need both the money and the new ideas that tourists bring.


### 2. Reading

Bali is a beautiful island of mountains, forests and rice fields. It is also an island of artists. Almost everybody there is an artist of some kind. The people work all day at home, in the fields or in their boats, but in the evenings they make music, dance, paint or carve things out of wood and stone. There are thousands of temples in Bali, and there are festivals at these temples when people die, or get married, or when a child is born.

Until the 1950's the Balinese people did not worry about the outside world. For them Bali was the whole world. But in the 1950's tourists began to visit Bali, and since then tourism has become big business there. So the people have begun to sell their art to tourists. These days the Balinese produce more and more things for tourists; they make fewer things for their temples. They have less time for their gods, so there are fewer festivals.

Every year more tourists bring more money to the island. They also bring new ideas and a new way of life. The Balinese need money, and they need new ideas. Nowadays too many people live on the island, and they need to buy food and other things from abroad. The Balinese know that if fewer tourists come to the island, there will be less money, too. But they also know that if there are too many tourists, the Balinese way of life will change too quickly. So the authorities are trying to organise and control tourism very carefully. Bali should change; but they want to make sure that it changes for the better, not for the worse.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. What does 'there' refer to in "Almost everybody there is an artist of some kind"?

2. What does 'them' refer to in "For them Bali was the whole world"?

3. What does 'They' refer to in "Every year more tourists bring more money to the island. They also bring new ideas and a new way of life"?

4. What does 'they' refer to in "But they also know that if there are too many tourists, the Balinese way of life will change too quickly"?

5. What does 'it' refer to in "Bali should change; but they want to make sure that it changes for the better, not for the worse"?


*B*

6. What does 'whole' mean in "For them Bali was the whole world"?

   - a) part

   - b) unusual

   - c) different

   - d) complete

7. What does 'produce' mean in "These days the Balinese produce more and more things for tourists"?

   - a) make

   - b) deliver

   - c) cause

   - d) include

8. What does 'abroad' mean in "they need to buy food and other things from abroad"?

   - a) salesmen

   - b) other countries

   - c) customers

   - d) one's own country


*C*

9. What do the Balinese people do in their free time?

10. When did the Balinese people begin to worry about the outside world?

11. Why do the Balinese have less time for their gods?

12. Why do the Balinese need tourists?

13. What will happen if tourists stop coming to Bali?

14. Why don't the Balinese authorities want too many tourists?


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Author's Purpose]* What is the main purpose of the passage?

   - A) To persuade tourists to stop visiting Bali in order to save its temples

   - B) To describe the geography and farming methods of Bali in detail

   - C) To show both the benefits and the risks of tourism in Bali and the need for careful control

   - D) To compare Balinese art with art from other islands

2. *[Inference]* What can be inferred about the impact of tourism on Bali since the 1950's?

   - A) Tourism has completely destroyed all religious festivals in Bali.

   - B) Tourism has created an economic dependence that competes with traditional religious life.

   - C) The Balinese authorities want to ban tourists from buying local art.

   - D) The population of Bali has decreased since the 1950's.

3. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In the final sentence, what does the phrase "for the better" mean?

   - A) in a positive way

   - B) more slowly

   - C) without spending money

   - D) like the 1950's


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Guided Summary: Tourism in Bali** (60-80 words): Write a short summary of how tourism has changed life in Bali based on the passage.

- **Task 2 — Opinion Paragraph: Benefits and Risks of Tourism** (80-110 words): Do you think tourism is good or bad for traditional places like Bali? Write a short paragraph explaining your opinion with reasons.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **carve** (*oymak, yontmak*): Local artists carve beautiful figures out of wood.

- **produce** (*üretmek*): Many factories produce goods for other countries.

- **abroad** (*yurt dışı, yurt dışında*): She wants to study abroad after high school.

- **authorities** (*yetkililer, idareciler*): City authorities are building a new park for families.

- **control** (*kontrol etmek, yönetmek*): The police try to control traffic in the busy center.

- **way of life** (*yaşam tarzı*): We should protect our traditional way of life.

## Passage 82: Life in Space

- **ID:** `raw1_082` | **Seviye:** `B1` | **Konu:** Astronomy & Space | **Sayfa:** 113, 114, 115
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_082_life_in_space.mp3` (*UK · en-GB-RyanNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. Observations of nearby stars suggest that ____ of them may have planets. *(İpucu: fraction / quantifier)*

2. The sun, a typical star, is ____ times more massive than the earth. *(İpucu: number)*

3. The programme called SETI will concentrate on studying ____ from stars that may have life-supporting planets. *(İpucu: two-word noun phrase)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. How did Dr. Campbell and his colleagues detect unseen planets?

   - A) By taking direct photographs of planets using radio waves.

   - B) By measuring small variations in a star's light caused by orbiting planets.

   - C) By launching the Space Telescope and Hipparchus into orbit.

   - D) By comparing the size of the sun with nearby galaxies.

2. According to Professor Roy, roughly how many stars in our galaxy might have life on them?

   - A) 100,000 million

   - B) 20,000 million

   - C) 10,000 million

   - D) 300,000 million


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Planets are easy to spot because they produce their own bright light.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — The SETI programme was founded in Canada by Dr. Bruce Campbell.


### 2. Reading

Hopes of finding life on other worlds have been raised by Canadian astronomers. Their observations of nearby stars have shown that half of them may have planets.

The discovery, announced at a recent meeting of the American Astronomical Society, provides important support for scientists who believe that planetary systems, and life, are common in our galaxy.

"These observations suggest that half our galaxy's 100,000 million stars have planets - and that means we must have a good chance of finding life out there," said Professor Archibald Roy of Glasgow University.

The research, carried out by Dr. Bruce Campbell of the Dominion Astronomical Observatory, Victoria, and Dr. Gordon Walker and Dr. Stephenson Yang of the University of British Columbia, Vancouver, employed a new astronomical measuring technique called high-resolution spectroscopy.

In the past, the search for other worlds has been limited by two factors. First, planets are tiny, i.e. very small, objects compared with stars; for instance, the sun, a typical star, is 300,000 times more massive or larger than the earth. Second, planets do not shine but only reflect (give back) light from stars.

But Dr. Campbell and his colleagues solved this problem by using high-resolution spectroscopy to measure variations in a star's light. Small differences in a star's light showed that unseen planets pushed and pulled the stars out of their paths, in other words, their ways.

The astronomers' results, published in the Science magazine showed that, of 16 nearby stars, two - Epsilon Eridani and Gamma Cephei - were definitely being affected by large bodies in orbit round them. Of the rest, five or six also appeared to have unseen companions.

The astronomers calculate that these unseen objects must be several hundred times more massive than Earth. However, they are almost certainly planets.

"There are about 100,000 million stars in our galaxy and about one fifth are stable, that is, unchanging and cool like our own sun," said Professor Roy. "Now it seems about half also have planets. That leaves us with 10,000 million stars which might have life on them. It's very encouraging."

Further evidence should follow, because in a few years two important space projects - the Space Telescope and a European satellite called Hipparchus - will be introduced. Both will increase scientists' powers to find out minute variations in stars' motions caused by orbiting planets.

In addition, a programme called the Search for Extraterrestrial Intelligence (SETI) will concentrate on studying radio waves from stars which may have life-supporting planets.

Today, many scientists believe that by the end of the century we may have an accurate answer to the fundamental question: are we alone?


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A*

1. What does 'Their' refer to in "Their observations of nearby stars have shown that half of them may have planets"?

2. What does 'them' refer to in "Their observations of nearby stars have shown that half of them may have planets"?

3. What does 'they' refer to in "The astronomers calculate that these unseen objects must be several hundred times more massive than Earth. However, they are almost certainly planets"?


*B*

4. In "employed a new astronomical measuring technique called high-resolution spectroscopy", 'high-resolution spectroscopy' is ____.

5. In "First, planets are tiny, i.e. very small, objects compared with stars; for instance, the sun, a typical star, is 300,000 times more massive or larger than the earth", the word 'tiny' is the opposite of ____.

6. In "the sun, a typical star, is 300,000 times more massive or larger than the earth", 'massive' means ____.

7. In "planets do not shine but only reflect (give back) light from stars", 'reflect' means ____.

8. In "pushed and pulled the stars out of their paths, in other words, their ways", 'paths' means ____.

9. In "about one fifth are stable, that is, unchanging and cool like our own sun", 'stable' means ____.


*C*

10. How many stars are there in our galaxy?

11. Which are bigger, stars or planets?

12. Give the names of the two stars mentioned in the article.

13. What are the two important space projects mentioned in the article?

14. The fifth paragraph is mainly about the differences between ____.


*D*

15. Write down the sentence in the passage that gives the following information: "About 20,000 million stars of our galaxy have characteristics similar to those of our sun."


*E*

16. Write (F) for the sentence expressing a fact and (O) for the one expressing an opinion: Slight differences in a star's light show that unseen planets in orbit round the star push and pull it out of its way.

17. Write (F) for the sentence expressing a fact and (O) for the one expressing an opinion: Today many scientists believe that by the end of the century we may have an exact answer to the fundamental question: are we alone?


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Main Idea]* What is the main idea of the passage?

   - A) To explain how the European satellite Hipparchus was built

   - B) To prove that Earth is the largest planet in our galaxy

   - C) To report new astronomical evidence suggesting that planets and life may be common in our galaxy

   - D) To criticize earlier astronomers for failing to find Epsilon Eridani

2. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In paragraph 10, what does the word "minute" in "minute variations in stars' motions" mean?

   - A) extremely small

   - B) last-minute

   - C) circular

   - D) sudden and violent

3. *[Detail]* Which of the following is supported by the astronomers' results published in Science magazine?

   - A) All 16 nearby stars had confirmed planets around them.

   - B) None of the 16 stars showed any change in their light.

   - C) Only Epsilon Eridani and Gamma Cephei are stable like our sun.

   - D) Roughly half of the 16 observed stars showed signs of orbiting companions.


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Summary: Searching for Other Worlds** (100-120 words): Summarize the main discovery described in the passage and explain how astronomers overcame the difficulty of finding planets around other stars.

- **Task 2 — Opinion Mini-Essay: Is the Search for Extraterrestrial Life Worthwhile?** (150-200 words): Is searching for life on other planets worth the time and money spent on space projects? Write a short opinion paragraph or mini-essay giving reasons for your view.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **observation** (*gözlem*): Careful observations of the night sky revealed a new comet.

- **massive** (*çok büyük, devasa*): Jupiter is a massive planet compared with Mars.

- **reflect** (*yansıtmak*): Calm water can reflect the light of the moon clearly.

- **orbit** (*yörünge*): The satellite will stay in orbit around Earth for ten years.

- **evidence** (*kanıt, delil*): Scientists need more evidence before they can confirm the theory.

- **fundamental** (*temel, esas*): Finding a cure for the disease remains a fundamental goal of medicine.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Ana fikri ve temel bilimsel kanıtları kendi kelimelerimle özetledim mi?

- [ ] Giriş, gelişme ve sonuç paragrafları arasında akıcı bağlantılar kullandım mı?

- [ ] Bilimsel terimleri (örneğin planet, orbit, evidence) doğru bağlamda kullandım mı?

- [ ] Kelime sınırına (100-120 / 150-200 kelime) ve dil bilgisi kurallarına dikkat ettim mi?

## Passage 83: Nice Not Easy

- **ID:** `raw1_083` | **Seviye:** `B1` | **Konu:** Au Pair Experience | **Sayfa:** 115, 116
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_083_nice_not_easy.mp3` (*US · en-US-JennyNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. After ____ weeks with the first family, Alison announced that she was leaving. *(İpucu: number)*

2. Alison lived above her second family's ____ for three happy months. *(İpucu: noun (type of shop))*

3. Using an agency can give au pairs the chance to meet others through a ____. *(İpucu: noun phrase)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. What surprised Alison about her work for the first family?

   - A) She only had to cook lunch for the children.

   - B) She worked four hours every morning and had afternoons free.

   - C) She had to do all the housework in a large villa instead of just light chores.

   - D) She was paid extra whenever she cleaned the bedrooms.

2. Why did the agent in Nice side with the employer against Alison?

   - A) Because Alison had broken expensive items in the villa.

   - B) Because the agent earned a fee for every new girl introduced.

   - C) Because the agent was a close relative of the family.

   - D) Because Alison did not speak any French.

3. According to Alison, what is one good reason to use an agency?

   - A) It can help au pairs meet other people through a language school.

   - B) It guarantees that every family will provide a house key.

   - C) It pays for the au pair's flights and visa fees.

   - D) It prevents employers from assigning housework.


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Madame H. always allowed Alison to eat whatever food she wanted in the villa.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Alison earned more money with her second family than with her first family.


### 2. Reading

Alison Bray takes a spot of French leave.

Every year thousands of girls from all over the world drop everything in their home country to become au pairs abroad. A need to improve language skills, discover other cultures, or simply find some independence are the most common reasons, but most have little idea of what is waiting for them.

When I landed in Nice last February, I was as green as the England I left behind. I was to work for a family in the "Nouveau Riche" mould just outside Nice and my contract stipulated caring for the children and "petite aide menagere" (light housework). I found, in fact, all the housework in the large villa was my responsibility.

A typical morning's work would involve washing and dressing the five-, seven- and nine-year-old children, preparing them for school, washing up the breakfast things, making the beds (to army standards), tidying their rooms and play rooms, cleaning and making the fire, vacuuming and washing either the upstairs or downstairs floor surfaces, polishing ornaments and all wood, cleaning the toilets and bathrooms, taking out and feeding the dog, setting the table and preparing part of the lunch.

The chores steadily mounted each week, so I was usually working from 7 a.m. until 9 p.m. with a two-hour break after lunch, six days a week, for £140 a month.

Despite Mme. H. agreeing on my contract that I would have "une vie de famille," I could not eat without her permission (she even locked away some things). I had to drink cheap sterilised milk rather than their "biological" milk and was left work on my day off. She would not wash my black or coloured clothes, I was refused a key, and only allowed one evening off a week... if I returned by 10 p.m.

Talking to other girls, I was convinced this was not right. After six weeks I announced I was going. Neither my agency in England nor Nice was ever of any help. The agent in Nice, at one stage, threatened me, believing my employer in preference to me: not surprising as they collected a nice fee for each new girl introduced.

I found my last family through a friend and spent three very happy months with them, living above their patisserie.

If you want to au pair, it is worthwhile using an agency if only to have the chance of meeting others through a language school, which they organise. Some countries require a visa. And, by the way, boys have been known to work as au pairs.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A. Mark the best choice.*

1. In "A need to improve language skills, discover other cultures, or simply find some independence are the most common reasons, but most have little idea of what is waiting for them.", what does 'most' refer to? Most ____.

   - a) cultures

   - b) language skills

   - c) reasons

   - d) girls

2. In "The agent in Nice, at one stage, threatened me, believing my employer in preference to me: not surprising as they collected a nice fee for each new girl introduced.", what does 'they' refer to?

   - a) the girls

   - b) the agent in Nice

   - c) the family

   - d) the agent in England


*B. Answer the following questions.*

3. What are the advantages of being an au pair abroad? (List three.)

4. Write two things that Mme. H. didn't let Alison do.


*C. Mark the statements as True (T) or False (F).*

5. For the first family Alison only looked after the children and did some light housework.

   - True

   - False

6. Alison had to work twelve hours a day, seven days a week.

   - True

   - False

7. Alison changed families after about six weeks.

   - True

   - False

8. The second family was not very kind.

   - True

   - False


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Vocabulary in Context]* What does Alison mean by saying "I was as green as the England I left behind"?

   - A) She missed the green countryside of England.

   - B) She felt sick during her flight to France.

   - C) She was completely inexperienced and naive.

   - D) She preferred wearing green clothes.

2. *[Main Idea]* What is the main contrast highlighted in Alison's account of her first placement?

   - A) She was angry about having to learn French at a language school.

   - B) Her daily working conditions were far stricter and harder than her contract promised.

   - C) The three children were too young to attend school.

   - D) She disliked living in the south of France because of the weather.

3. *[Inference]* What can be inferred from the passage about Alison's contact with other au pairs?

   - A) Most au pairs work in patisseries rather than private homes.

   - B) Au pairs are legally forbidden from speaking to other au pairs.

   - C) Agencies always protect au pairs when contracts are broken.

   - D) Talking with other au pairs helped Alison realize her treatment was unacceptable.


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Summary of Alison's Experience** (80-100 words): Write a short summary of Alison's experience as an au pair in France, contrasting her first and second placements.

- **Task 2 — Pros and Cons of Working as an Au Pair** (110-140 words): Do you think working as an au pair abroad is a good idea for young people? Explain the benefits and risks, and suggest how to avoid problems.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **stipulate** (*şart koşmak, hükme bağlamak*): My contract stipulated that I would work thirty hours a week.

- **chores** (*günlük ev işleri*): Daily chores like washing dishes and vacuuming take a lot of time.

- **steadily mount** (*giderek artmak, birikmek*): Complaints about the long hours steadily mounted over the month.

- **permission** (*izin, müsaade*): She could not leave the house late without her parents' permission.

- **worthwhile** (*değer, zahmete değer*): It is worthwhile learning a few basic phrases before travelling abroad.

- **in preference to** (*...e tercih ederek, ...den ziyade*): In preference to staying indoors, we decided to go for a walk.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Metindeki olumlu ve olumsuz deneyimleri açıkça özetledim.

- [ ] Zaman kiplerini (özellikle past simple) tutarlı şekilde kullandım.

- [ ] Paragraf içi geçiş ifadeleri (however, despite, although vb.) ekledim.

- [ ] Yazım, noktalama ve kelime sayısı sınırını kontrol ettim.

## Passage 84: The Olympic Marathon

- **ID:** `raw1_084` | **Seviye:** `A2` | **Konu:** History of the Marathon | **Sayfa:** 117, 118
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_084_the_olympic_marathon.mp3` (*AU · en-AU-NatashaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. A famous war was fought in the Greek village of Marathon in ____. *(İpucu: historical year)*

2. Only ____ athletes from twelve countries took part in the 1896 Olympic Games in Athens. *(İpucu: number)*

3. Female athletes competed in the Olympic Games for the first time in ____ in Amsterdam. *(İpucu: year)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. How often and where did the ancient Greeks hold the Olympic festival?

   - A) Every year in Athens

   - B) Once every four years in Olympia

   - C) Every two years in Marathon

   - D) Once every five years in Cyprus

2. Why was the marathon added to the first modern Olympic Games?

   - A) To test which country had the fastest soldiers

   - B) To replace older running races in the festival

   - C) To remember a Greek soldier's run from Marathon to Athens

   - D) To celebrate the end of a modern war in Europe

3. Why do some people view the modern Olympics as a "political marathon"?

   - A) Governments pay for athletes' expenses because they want them to win.

   - B) Only professional sportsmen are allowed to compete now.

   - C) Athletes no longer have to train before the competition.

   - D) Countries stop their wars whenever the games begin.


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — The ancient Greeks paused their wars so the Olympic Games could take place.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Modern Olympic athletes earn more money than professional sportsmen.


### 2. Reading

Sport is full of wonderful moments, but perhaps nothing is as exciting as the finish of the marathon. It is the longest, hardest race of all. The name 'marathon' comes from a village in Greece. A famous war was fought there in the year 490 BC. When the Greeks won the war, a soldier ran all the way from Marathon to Athens (more than 40 km.) The organisers of the first modern Olympic games in 1896 decided to include the marathon in the games so that this event would not be forgotten. The marathon has been a race since then.

At first the Olympic Games were part of a festival. The Greeks had this festival once in every four years in Olympia. Athletes from Greece, Cyprus, Sicily, etc. went there to participate in the games. These games were very important to the Greeks. They even stopped their wars for them.

The modern Olympic Games were started again in 1896 in Athens. However, only 300 athletes from 12 countries went there to participate in the games. Since then, of course, the games have become much more popular. Only male athletes participated in the early Olympic Games. In the 1928 Olympics in Amsterdam an important change took place. Female athletes participated for the first time.

We all know that only amateur athletes can participate in the Olympic Games. An amateur is someone who doesn't earn any money from sport. But today it is difficult to say who is an amateur and who is not. It is true that Olympic athletes never earn as much money as professional sportsmen. But they are often students or teachers of a sport and have to spend a lot of time training. Their governments pay for their training, travel and pocket-money, because they want them to win. Some people think that this changes the Olympics. They feel that the games are now a political marathon.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A. What do these words refer to?*

1. In "Sport is full of wonderful moments, but perhaps nothing is as exciting as the finish of the marathon. It is the longest, hardest race of all.", what does 'It' refer to?

2. In "The Greeks had this festival once in every four years in Olympia. Athletes from Greece, Cyprus, Sicily, etc. went there to participate in the games.", what does 'there' refer to?

3. In "These games were very important to the Greeks. They even stopped their wars for them.", what does 'them' refer to?

4. In "The modern Olympic Games were started again in 1896 in Athens. However, only 300 athletes from 12 countries went there to participate in the games.", what does 'there' refer to?

5. In "The modern Olympic Games were started again in 1896 in Athens. ... Since then, of course, the games have become much more popular.", what does 'then' refer to?

6. In "It is true that Olympic athletes never earn as much money as professional sportsmen. But they are often students or teachers of a sport...", what does 'they' refer to?


*B. Mark the best choice.*

7. The word 'marathon' was first used for a ____.

   - a) war

   - b) story

   - c) place

   - d) race

8. The 'marathon' race was included in the modern Olympic Games to remember that ____.

   - a) the Greeks had a war in Marathon

   - b) the distance between Marathon and Athens was run by a Greek soldier

   - c) there was a race called 'marathon' in the early Olympic Games

   - d) the Olympic Games were started in Marathon

9. Some people think that the games are now a political marathon because ____.

   - a) Olympic athletes earn a lot of money

   - b) the governments spend money on their athletes

   - c) Olympic athletes spend a lot of time preparing for the games

   - d) only amateurs can participate in the games


*C. Answer the following questions.*

10. In the passage, "When the Greeks won the war, a soldier ran all the way from Marathon to Athens (more than 40 km.)", what was the good news the soldier ran to tell the people?

11. How long has the marathon been a race in the Olympics?

12. How many countries participated in the first modern Olympics?

13. How long have there been female athletes in the Olympics?


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Vocabulary in Context]* According to the passage, what is an "amateur" athlete?

   - A) A soldier who runs long distances for a country

   - B) An athlete who wins a medal in every festival

   - C) Someone who plays a sport without earning money from it

   - D) A teacher who trains only female athletes

2. *[Detail]* How have the modern Olympic Games changed since 1896?

   - A) They were moved permanently to Amsterdam.

   - B) Fewer countries send athletes now than in 1896.

   - C) Wars are still stopped globally during the games.

   - D) They have grown in popularity and expanded to include women.

3. *[Author's Purpose]* What point does the author make in the final paragraph?

   - A) Government financial support makes it harder to keep the games purely amateur and non-political.

   - B) Professional athletes should not be allowed to watch the Olympic Games.

   - C) The marathon distance should be shortened to under 40 kilometres.

   - D) Teaching sport at schools is more important than competing in the Olympics.


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Origin and Growth of the Olympic Marathon** (70-90 words): Write a short summary explaining the origin of the marathon and how the modern Olympic Games have changed over time.

- **Task 2 — Government Support for Olympic Athletes** (80-110 words): Should governments pay for Olympic athletes' training and travel expenses? Give your opinion and explain your reasons.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **participate in** (*katılmak*): Thousands of runners participate in the city marathon every spring.

- **amateur** (*amatör, profesyonel olmayan*): She is an amateur photographer who takes pictures just for fun.

- **professional** (*profesyonel*): Professional athletes earn money by competing in tournaments.

- **organiser** (*organizatör, düzenleyen kişi*): The organisers prepared water stations along the whole race route.

- **pocket-money** (*cep harçlığı*): Children often receive a small amount of pocket-money each week.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Maratonun kökenini ve Olimpiyat Oyunları'nın tarihçesini net cümlelerle aktardım.

- [ ] Tarih ve yer bilgilerini (490 BC, 1896, 1928) doğru kullandım.

- [ ] Ana fikri kendi cümlelerimle özetledim.

- [ ] Temel dilbilgisi ve noktalama hatalarını düzelttim.

## Passage 85: The Technological Revolution

- **ID:** `raw1_085` | **Seviye:** `B1` | **Konu:** Technology & Automation | **Sayfa:** 119, 120
- **Ses Dosyası:** `../audio/raw1/raw1_085_the_technological_revolution.mp3` (*UK · en-GB-SoniaNeural · Hız -4%*)

### 1. Listening (Metni Okumadan Dinleyerek Çözün)

**A. Gap-Fill (Boşluk Doldurma)**

1. By the year ____, a large amount of our food could be produced by new farming machines. *(İpucu: year)*

2. Using satellite transmission, office computers send ____ to customers all around the world. *(İpucu: plural noun)*

3. The size of the human brain has not changed over the past ____ years. *(İpucu: number)*


**B. Listening Multiple Choice**

1. How have farmers been using computers according to the passage?

   - A) To replace tractors and threshers in the fields

   - B) To process data on soil chemistry, insect control, and plant genetics

   - C) To send letters to customers by satellite transmission

   - D) To design the shapes of new farm buildings

2. What benefit do computerized robots bring to industrial workers?

   - A) Robots allow factories to close earlier every day.

   - B) Workers can take over the jobs of company accountants.

   - C) Workers are freed from physical work to use their brains for new discoveries.

   - D) Robots are able to design car shapes without engineers.

3. What do computer graphics help engineers do in the car industry?

   - A) Designing car shapes and calculating engine part sizes

   - B) Typing letters and sending bills to drivers

   - C) Driving finished cars off the assembly lines

   - D) Monitoring soil conditions around car factories


**C. True / False / Not Given**

1. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Computers can perform calculations much faster than the human brain.

2. [ ] True  [ ] False  [ ] Not Given — Computerized robots have caused higher unemployment across all industries.


### 2. Reading

From the invention of the first stone tools to today's complex computers, man has developed the power to change the world around him. Computer technology could be taking over our economic life.

On the farm, modern tractors and threshers have taken the place of field workers. With their help, one man can plant and harvest wheat and corn to feed thousands of people. Farmers have been using computers to process information about soil chemistry, insect control and plant genetics. By the year 2000, much of the food we eat could be the result of these new machines.

In business, computers do the job of the accountants and secretaries. In minutes, office computers type out hundreds of letters and, by satellite transmission, send out bills to customers around the world. Business managers use computers to collect huge amounts of information and, in seconds, to do calculations beyond the power of the human brain. In the future, the most important decision-maker might be the computer, not the company.

In industry, computerized robots have replaced many workers on factory assembly lines. Because technology supplies the muscle power, workers are free to use their brain power to make new discoveries. In the automobile industry, computer graphics help engineers design the shape of the car and calculate the size of engine parts.

The human brain has not changed in size in the past 100,000 years. However, man was able to invent hand tools, then the steam engine and now the computer. Man has used his brain to create 'intelligent machines' to do his work for him.


**A. Kitaptaki Orijinal Sorular**


*A. Mark the statements as True (T) or False (F).*

1. The invention of the tractor and thresher has resulted in more jobs for farm workers.

   - True

   - False

2. The computer solves mathematical problems that man can't.

   - True

   - False

3. Computer graphics help engineers design parts of cars.

   - True

   - False

4. The human brain has increased in size in the past 100,000 years.

   - True

   - False


*B. Answer the following questions.*

5. For what purpose have farmers been using computers?

6. What is the function of computers in the field of business?

7. What is one advantage that the computerized robots provide for workers?


*C. Reference questions.*

8. In "From the invention of the first stone tools to today's complex computers, man has developed the power to change the world around him.", what does 'him' refer to?

9. In "On the farm, modern tractors and threshers have taken the place of field workers. With their help, one man can plant and harvest wheat and corn...", what does 'their help' refer to the help of?

10. In "Because technology supplies the muscle power, workers are free to use their brain power to make new discoveries.", what does 'their' refer to?


**B. Ekstra Sınav Soruları (Proficiency / IELTS / TOEFL)**

1. *[Main Idea]* What is the main topic of the passage?

   - A) How ancient stone tools were made by early humans

   - B) How computers and modern machines are transforming farming, business, and industry

   - C) Why the human brain stopped growing 100,000 years ago

   - D) Why farmers prefer traditional tools over modern tractors

2. *[Vocabulary in Context]* In the fourth paragraph, what does the phrase "muscle power" refer to?

   - A) Medical care for injured factory workers

   - B) Electrical energy generated by steam engines

   - C) Special training for company managers

   - D) Physical strength and manual labour

3. *[Inference]* What does the final paragraph suggest about human intelligence?

   - A) Human creativity does not depend on the brain growing physically larger.

   - B) Intelligent machines will soon replace the human brain completely.

   - C) The steam engine was more important than the computer.

   - D) People 100,000 years ago already knew how to build simple computers.


### 3. Writing

- **Task 1 — Technology Across Three Sectors** (80-100 words): Summarize how modern machines and computers are changing agriculture, business, and industry according to the passage.

- **Task 2 — Machines Replacing Human Workers** (130-170 words): Do intelligent machines and robots do more harm than good by replacing human workers, or do they bring more benefits to society? Discuss both sides and give your opinion.


**Useful Vocabulary & Collocations:**

- **take the place of** (*yerini almak*): Electric vehicles may soon take the place of petrol cars.

- **process information** (*bilgi işlemek*): The lab uses powerful computers to process information from weather satellites.

- **assembly line** (*montaj hattı*): Robots work quickly and accurately on car assembly lines.

- **decision-maker** (*karar verici*): She is the main decision-maker in the marketing department.

- **brain power** (*zihin gücü, beyin gücü*): Solving this puzzle requires a lot of brain power.

- **satellite transmission** (*uydu yayını, uydu iletimi*): Live television reaches viewers via satellite transmission.


**Self-Assessment Checklist:**

- [ ] Tarım, iş dünyası ve sanayi alanlarındaki teknolojik değişimleri ayrı ayrı belirttim.

- [ ] Paragraflar arasında mantıklı bağlantı ve akış sağladım.

- [ ] Tekrarlardan kaçınarak eş anlamlı kelimeler kullandım.

- [ ] Dilbilgisi, yazım ve kelime sayısı hedeflerine uydum.
